A block longer than the rest of the timeline is still cut at the end of the timeline, but a warning, printed once per run, names the timeset, the block, the timeline and the number of time steps not used; on a representative-period timeset this is an error (before, the run crashed later with an internal error). A timeset whose timeline is not a timeline of the scenario is an error. Only the timesets used by the solves of the model are checked. A block length that is not a whole number gets a warning. No change to the model otherwise.
    • A time map without index_name of a node penalty_up / penalty_down / availability / self_discharge_loss / storage_state_reference_value or of an other_operational_cost added its time steps to the model's periods. It now reads only as a time series, as the same map with index_name time does.
    • A run reloaded from edited solve_data files left out the operating costs of a period or time step without a word when the inflation factor, the share of the year, the time step duration or the time step weight had no value for it; it now stops with an error that names the file and the missing periods or time steps.
    • A profile map without index_name keyed by periods was read as a time series and gave 0. A map such as p2020: 0.3, p2025: 0.35 now gives each period its value in every time step, as the same map with index_name period does (single, rolling, nested and stochastic solves). A map whose keys are both periods and time steps of the model (or a key that is both) stops the run with an error naming the profile and the alternative: set its index_name to period or time. A profile period map (with or without index_name) that leaves out periods of the solves the scenario runs gets one warning per run naming the profile and those periods; the profile is 0 there.
    • A two-level (period + time) profile map with an unnamed level gave 0. Two shapes were affected: a map without index_name on its outer level (its inner level with or without index_name time), which was taken for a stochastic map (those have three levels), and a map with index_name period on its outer level but none on its inner level, whose time steps were not read. Either way the profile was 0 in every time step without a word. Both now read as the same map with index_name period and time (item 223), when the outer keys are periods (the stochastic branch periods take their anchor period's curve), in single, rolling and stochastic solves. Unnamed outer keys that are time steps, both periods and time steps, or no period, and unnamed inner keys that are periods, stop the run with an error naming the profile and the alternative; a period missing from the map gets the warning above. Three-level (stochastic) maps are unchanged.

    User constraints

    • A user constraint without capacity terms lost its constant when another constraint had one (since 4.0). When some constraint used constraint_cumulative_pre_built_capacity_coeff or constraint_capacity_coeff (whose existing capacity moves to the right-hand side) or, in a chain of solves with a construction lead time, constraint_invested_capacity_coeff or constraint_in_construction_capacity_coeff (capacity an earlier solve decided), and constant was a single value, every constraint without such a term got a right-hand side of 0 instead of its constant (e.g. flow ≤ 800 became flow ≤ 0), in per-step and once-per-period rows alike. Models without these terms, or with a period-map constant, are unchanged.

    Outputs and plots

    • VRE curtailment, VRE potential and flow-group energies per period are scaled to a full year. unit_curtailment__outputNode__d, unit_outputNode_VRE_potential__d and the cumulative_flow of group_flow__d were sums over the sampled time steps (the flow group's also without the timestep weights); they are now MWh per year, weighted and annualised like the flows, so with a 2-day period they are 182.5 times larger. The curtailment share is unchanged; average_flow is unchanged with uniform timestep weights and is now the weighted mean with timeset weights (before, it ignored them). The "total" plots multiply each period by its years represented. New tables group_node__horizon and group_node_VRE_share__horizon: the node-group indicators and the VRE share over the whole horizon as ratios of the total energies (each period scaled to a year and times its years represented), not averages of the period shares; the "total" plots use them. Plot labels say what the numbers are: "(annualized)", "(horizon total)", costs in "M CUR/a (annualized)", "M CUR (horizon total, undiscounted)" and "M CUR (discounted)", CO2 in "Mt CO2 (horizon total)".
    • The inertia result tables were always empty. group_inertia__dt and group__unit__node_inertia__dt had no value column; they now hold the inertia of each group's units (flow × inertia_constant). The model was right.
    • The unit of a storage node's annuity. entity_annuity_d_p holds units, connections and storage nodes in one table. A storage node's capacity is an amount (how much it holds), so its annuity is per amount unit: CUR/MWh (was CUR/MW), or CUR/m3 for a node whose unit_of_measure is m3. A unit's or connection's capacity is a rate, so its annuity stays per rate unit (CUR/MW, or CUR/(m3/h)). This changes the storage-node columns' unit in the parquet footer, the Excel unit row, Units and _output_metadata sheets, units.csv and datapackage.json; values are unchanged. The combined scenario-comparison parquets now carry the units of each scenario's columns in their footer, as the scenarios' own parquets do.
    • The slack lines of summary_solve.csv are weighted like the objective. The Created / Removed / Inertia / NonSync / Reserve lines summed the slack over the time steps × duration without the timestep weights, so with timeset_weights or representative periods they did not match the penalty the objective charges. They are now Σ w·Δt·slack per period (not annualised, as before). Uniform weights give the same numbers; in a representative-period solve the line is now on the scale of the represented sample. The CapMargin line (per period) is unchanged.
    • The per-period result tables of a rolling solve were labelled with one roll (#210). The solve column of the …__d tables (CSV, Excel, results database) named the last roll that realized a part of the period (dispatch_fullYear_roll_roll_17). It now names the complete solve (dispatch_fullYear_roll). The capacity tables (unit_capacity__d, connection_capacity__d, node_capacity__d) and the investment marginal values name the solve that realized the period's investments, for example the investment solve of an investment → dispatch chain, instead of the dispatch solve. The per-step tables are unchanged. Values do not change.
    • An Excel sheet name over 31 characters lost its time-resolution suffix. Excel cuts sheet names at 31 characters, so unit_curtailment__outputNode__dt became the sheet unit_curtailment__outputNode__d and the real per-period table unit_curtailment__outputNode_1. A long name now keeps its __d / __dt suffix and is shortened in front of it, with _1, _2, … before the suffix when two names would be the same (also without regard to case, as Excel compares them). The shipped long names get hand-made sheet names instead (process__reserve__node__dt, process__reserve__node__d_avg, reserve_price__group__dt, slack__reserve__group__d / __dt, unit_VRE_potential__d / __dt, unit_VRE_curtailment_share__d / __dt), and the curtailment table unit_curtailment__outputNode__d / __dt gets the sheet name unit_VRE_curtailment__d / __dt like its neighbours; the CSV file names do not change. The scenario-comparison workbook and the dispatch-data workbook name their sheets the same way.
    • The comparison dispatch plots added together the time steps of the same name in different periods. In a multi-period scenario each step (pv, loss of load, curtailment, demand, ...) was the sum over the periods, in the order of the step names. The periods now follow one another in the realized order, with a gap where a period does not continue the previous one, and the x axis names the time steps with the period below them. In a comparison of scenarios with different steps, each scenario shows its own steps in its own order (not zero rows for the other scenarios' steps), and its plot has gaps only where its own timeline breaks. The same fix applies to the dispatch plots in the result viewer. Output format: the plotted-data CSV of a dispatch plot and dispatch_data.xlsx now have a period column before time.
    • The period node-group indicators and VRE share were not shares of the period's energy. Curtailed VRE of potential VRE was divided by the inflow instead of the potential VRE generation and, per period, summed over the time steps (so it could exceed 1); Curtailed VRE of demand was an average of the time-step shares; nodeGroup_VRE_share_d_g was an average of the time-step shares and VRE share of demand lost the VRE of a time step without inflow. Every period share (group indicator, nodeGroup_VRE_share_d_g) is now the ratio of the period sums weighted by the time-step weights, so the values of these results change. The VRE share takes the VRE generation into all member nodes, as the indicators do; the curtailed VRE is taken on the arcs that have a potential (VRE units with an upper_limit profile), so the flow of a VRE arc without a profile no longer offsets it; the time-step VRE share is 0 (not infinite) at a time step without inflow; a realized time step without a time-step weight stops the output processing with an error.
    • The reported input of a unit with a profile (unit__node__profile on a unit that also has an input) equalled its output: the efficiency was left out. Its fuel or electricity use, the node results at its input node, and the commodity cost, CO2 emissions and CO2 cost computed from that input (per unit, per group and the calculated total cost) were too low. The optimisation itself was right (the solver objective in summary_solve.csv was correct).
    • Line and stacked-area plots leave a gap where there are no results. A time step whose plotted cells are all empty, and the gap at a timeline break, were drawn as 0 in a line that sums several columns and in every stacked-area plot (scenario, comparison and result-viewer plots); a time step with no result at all was dropped, so the line joined across it. They are now gaps. In a sum, an empty cell next to values adds nothing. Bar charts are unchanged (an empty value draws no bar). The scenario comparison's dispatch plots now leave a gap at a timeline break too (before, they drew it as 0).

    Databases and the FlexTool window

    • The model run refuses an input database that is not at the current FlexTool version. Before, only databases below version 25 were refused, so an unmigrated database ran old data under the new version's semantics without a word. flextool-run (and run_flextool.py, python -m flextool.cli.cmd_run_flextool) now stops with a message that names flextool-migrate-database <file>; a database newer than FlexTool asks for a FlexTool update. The standard ways to run migrate first: execute_flextool_workflow.py migrates the input database before the model run (new), the FlexTool window migrates its sources on refresh and now also checks the sources of the scenarios added to the execution list, and the Spine Toolbox workflow runs its Migrate database tool before FlexTool.
    • Checking the version of an input source no longer upgrades its SpineDB schema. The FlexTool window read the version by opening each .sqlite source with a schema upgrade, so an external file's schema changed before the consent dialog was shown. The version and the SpineDB schema revision are now read read-only; a source at the current FlexTool version with an older SpineDB schema (e.g. after a spinedb_api update) is migrated like an older one (an internal source quietly, an external one after asking, a frozen one through the frozen dialog), and the model run reports such a database with the migrate command instead of a SpineDB error.
    • A canonical example database left at an older version is rebuilt. templates/examples.sqlite, the time-settings template and the how-to example databases are rebuilt from their JSON sources when the file in the FlexTool folder is older than this FlexTool (the old file is kept as <name>.v<version>.bak); before, an existing file was never refreshed, and the model run now refuses it. Calibrate investments migrates its scenarios' sources first, as a normal run does.
    • The Excel export of an input database put a flowGroup's max_cumulative_flow / min_cumulative_flow on the time-series sheets; they are constant or per period and are now on the period sheets.
    • The Solver options dialog said the .opt files are in the project's solver_config folder. They are in the solver_config folder of the FlexTool workspace (or $FLEXTOOL_SOLVER_CONFIG_DIR).
    • The Spine Toolbox tool specifications listed files that no longer exist (bin/highs.opt of the FlexTool tool, old modules of Process outputs and scenario comparison). Toolbox skipped them without a word; they are removed.

    FlexTool 2 import

    • FlexTool 2 invested capacity (MW) / invested storage (MWh) are imported as existing capacity. They are capacity already built in FlexTool 2. The importer wrote them as a forced investment, which a dispatch-only workbook (mode_invest 0) never made, nor any solve for an entity without an investment cost, so that capacity was lost; with capacity (MW) empty it was also written as existing, so counted twice; and a max invest beside it was dropped. Now existing is capacity + invested capacity (units, storage, connections, also in sensitivities) and max invest stays the investment limit. FlexTool 2 adds the annuity of that capacity to its total cost, FlexTool 4 does not: the import report lists the entities that have an investment cost (base data and sensitivities). A sensitivity that sets invested storage (MWh) or storage (MWh) to 0 keeps a storage that can still invest (max invest (MWh)) a storage node; before, it became a balance node and lost the investment.
    • A FlexTool 2 storage with only one max invest can invest. FlexTool 2 derives the other limit through the fixed kW/kWh ratio (MW = ratio × MWh); the import left that side without an investment option, so the ratio link held both at 0. Now both limits are written (also when a sensitivity changes one). A sensitivity on inv.cost/kWh sets the storage node's cost (before: the discharger's cost per kW). A storage that can invest in MWh with neither a cost per kWh nor a ratio is listed in the import report: FlexTool 2 adds it at no cost, FlexTool 4 does not invest in it.
    • An annuity typed over the FlexTool 2 workbook's formula is listed in the import report. FlexTool 4 computes the annuity from invest_cost, discount_rate and lifetime and ignores that column.
    • The FlexTool 2 fixed kW/kWh ratio was inverted. FlexTool 2 sets invested MW = ratio × invested MWh; the import wrote ratio × MW = MWh (a ratio of 0.25 gave a 1/16 h storage instead of a 4 h one).
    • The FlexTool 2 importer (flextool-read-old, GUI "Convert from FlexTool 2.0 input file") translates more and reports what it cannot. Sensitivities are now imported in the same run as the base data (--master MASTER.xlsm; in the GUI the master workbook is asked for before the conversion), before the database is migrated: before, the GUI wrote them into an already migrated database, where a renamed parameter such as the capacity margin was dropped with only a log line. A target that is a FlexTool database at another version than the import's (56) is refused, as is flextool-import-sensitivities on a migrated database. The FlexTool 2 master workbook given as the input file stops with a message naming the right files (before: an empty database). fix unit generation, use min generation and use max generation with their ts_unit series (shares of capacity) become profiles with profile_method fixed, lower_limit and upper_limit on the unit's primary output (before: ignored, so must-run units were free). storage start / storage finish set storage_start_end_method explicitly (fix_start, fix_end, fix_start_end, else fix_nothing): before, a storage start of 0 fixed the first state at 0 (FlexTool 2 leaves it free), a storage finish was not read, and with both set the storage still wrapped from the last step to the first (now bind_forward_only, as in FlexTool 2). The import ends with a report of failed writes and of every FlexTool 2 input that was not carried over or works differently, with its entities and what to do, grouped by topic in plain sentences (flextool-read-old --strict makes a failed write an error), and the imported database carries no behaviour-change notices.
    • FlexTool 2 CHP units (output2) are imported as FlexTool 2 defines them. The output2 (e.g. heat) used fuel and shared the unit's capacity with the primary output, and output2 max capacity ratio was divided by the capacity and written as conversion_flow_coeff, which changed the fuel use. The output2 eq / gt / lt rows had output1 and output2 swapped (heat >= 1.4 × elec became elec >= 1.4 × heat), their constants were not multiplied by the capacity, and a row with only a constant was dropped. Now the output2 edge has conversion_flow_coeff 0 (no fuel, outside the capacity, ramp and minimum-load limits, as in FlexTool 2), and each row, and the capacity ratio, becomes a user constraint output2 − coeff × output1 {=, ≥, ≤} constant × capacity in service. Headers written with underscores (output2_max_capacity_ratio) are read.
    • An invested FlexTool 2 CHP keeps its output2 in the dispatch solve. The output2 constraints counted only the capacity invested in the same solve, so in the dispatch solve after the investment solve the heat was limited to 0. They now use the capacity in service (pre-built + invested). The import report notes an output2 without any row (free heat).
    • In older FlexTool 2 workbooks the output2 constants are MW. A workbook with the output2 max capacity (MW) column (in place of output2 max capacity ratio) gives the output2 eq / gt / lt constant in MW; the importer multiplied them by the unit's capacity (a constant of 100 on a 50 MW unit became 5000 MW). They are now written as MW, with no capacity term, and the import report says so under "How some inputs were read". Newer workbooks are unchanged (constants per unit of capacity).
    • FlexTool 2 availability no longer derates dispatch. FlexTool 2 uses it only in the capacity margin; the import wrote it as FlexTool 4 availability, which reduces every flow of the unit. It is now left out and listed in the import report (FlexTool 4 has no per-unit share in the capacity margin).
    • A FlexTool 2 sensitivity that turns use_ramps off is imported (it wrote a ramp method the import's database version does not have, and the import stopped).
    • A FlexTool 2 workbook with no time step in use is refused with a message. When no ts_time step has in_use (or in_use_invest for an investment run) 1, the import stopped with a SpineDB error after creating the database; now it says which column is empty, before writing anything.
    • The FlexTool 2 import report flags a time_in_years that does not match the time series. FlexTool 2 scales operating costs by the years the series represents, FlexTool 4 by the hours it covers; the report now compares time_in_years with steps × duration / 8760 (before: with 1, which missed a two-week workbook with time_in_years = 1 and flagged a consistent two-year one) and gives the factor by which the ratio of operating to investment costs differs, with the remedy. It also lists cf and inflow series that FlexTool 2 rescales over the steps in use. In the FlexTool window, a converted FlexTool 2 model is written outside input_sources and moved in only after the conversion, and the new source and its scenarios are ticked again. The CLI now prints its log, and a failed import leaves no database file behind.

    Examples

    • The demand how-to example (demand.sqlite) served its demand only from the penalty slack. Its coal plant was not active in any scenario and had no capacity, and the scale_to_peak_and_annual scenario asked for peaks of 500 / 600 / 700 MW that cannot reach the annual flows (the scaled demand went above the peak). The coal plant now has 3500 MW and is active in the coal alternative, and the peaks are 1500 / 2200 / 3000 MW. The example's results change.
    • The battery of the non-synchronous how-to example was a node without anything connected in the wind scenario. The battery node is now active only in the battery alternative, like its connection. The wind scenario no longer has the idle node; its results do not change otherwise.
    • Getting the corrected example databases. A how to example databases/Demand.sqlite or non_sync_and_curtailment.sqlite left by FlexTool 4.3 or older is rebuilt from the new data when the FlexTool window starts or FlexTool is updated (see "A canonical example database left at an older version is rebuilt" above; the old file is kept as <name>.v<version>.bak). A file already at this FlexTool's version is never replaced, so your edits are kept: to get the corrected copy, rename or delete the file and start the FlexTool window again (or run python -m flextool.update_flextool.canonical_databases materialize in the FlexTool folder).

    AI assistant

    • A tool call the AI service could not read is asked again at once, not waited for. With gpt-oss on Ollama, Ollama sometimes answered "error parsing tool call": the model had written its reasoning into the tool call. The assistant took that for a busy server, waited up to five minutes and said a model was likely being loaded. It now asks the model again at once, at most twice, and then says that the service could not read a tool call in the model's answer: ask again, or try another model or level. The assistant also sends the model's own reasoning back to Ollama between the tool calls of a question, as gpt-oss expects, which keeps the reasoning out of its tool calls; the reasoning is never shown and is kept with the saved conversation. When the model lists the runs of one state, it now picks from FlexTool's run states (it guessed names such as "success" before).

    Docs

    • Planning-practitioner pages. New pages Reading results (where to look first, units) and Model limitations; a tutorial step that lets the model invest; how-tos for a minimum inertia (with a synchronous condenser), a sensitivity as one alternative on top of a scenario, replacing a dataset through a partial Excel import (--no-purge), several milestones with representative-period investment and full-year dispatch, and a terminal batch loop (bash and PowerShell); Advanced concepts says how costs are discounted between milestones (set inflation_rate to the social discount rate in a real-terms model). Fix (docs): the fixed cost of new capacity counts only within the model horizon, not over its full lifetime as Advanced concepts said, and the solve-sequence how-to used old names for rolling_solve_jump, rolling_solve_horizon, contains_solves and fix_storage_periods. Results and Reading results now give invest_marginal in CUR/kW (CUR/kWh for a storage), the unit of invest_cost and of the result files; they said per MW.
    • How-to "How to make a unit run all the time (must-run)" (Thermal, CHP and reserves). Six ways to make a unit or a fleet run (profile lower limit, a user constraint on a flow or on a fleet's output, a flowGroup min_instant_flow, a demand that needs the unit's other product, a user constraint on the units online) with when to use each, an exact online count (constraint_online_coeff with sense equal), why min_load alone is not a must-run and why a negative cost is a poor must-run bonus. Eight example scenarios must_run_* in examples.sqlite.
    • How-to "How to compare emission trajectories" (Emissions and system limits): one co2_max_period trajectory per alternative on the same four-period system, without, with existing and with additional measures, and how to compare them. Three example scenarios co2_*_measures in examples.sqlite.
    • How-to "How to model cogeneration with desalination" (Thermal, CHP and reserves): a water node in m³, a thermal desalination unit fed by a backpressure CHP, the same with an extraction CHP whose operating region is made of user constraints, a reverse-osmosis investment (an invest_cost per m³/h is entered ÷ 1000) and a water storage that lets the plant run flat. Four example scenarios desal_* in examples.sqlite.
    • How-to "How to model CSP with thermal storage" (Investment and multi-year models): a solar field, a heat storage and a turbine; the solar multiple and the storage hours as user constraints on the capacities (with the design efficiency and constraint_capacity_coeff), an optimised design, a gas backup, a plant summary (field potential and curtailment, capacity factor, tank cycles, levelised cost) from the existing outputs, and the limitations. Five example scenarios csp_* in examples.sqlite.
    • The CHP how-to gave the coefficient 2 on the electricity output of coal_chp_fix; the example has 1 (with −0.5 on heat, so heat = 2 × electricity). The how-to also says how the coal_chp_extraction alternative builds its operating area.
    • The ramp-limit text in Units no longer names the internal p_fix_online as something a user sets; it names the online status a stochastic realization fixes and a user constraint on the units online.
    • A unit efficiency may be above 1 (no change in results). A heat pump with COP 3 has efficiency 3; a desalination unit with an electricity input and a water output in m³ uses the m³ per MWh ratio (e.g. 250). The database upgrade to version 79 rewrites the efficiency and efficiency_at_min_load descriptions to say so.
    • The user-constraint capacity coefficients (constraint_capacity_coeff, ..._invested_capacity_coeff, and the other constraint_*capacity_coeff of unit, node and connection) say in their description that they are a map of coefficients indexed by constraint name (description only).
    • Solver pages. Solver selection gives the one option precedence for every solver, where the option files come from (new section) and what happens at a time limit; the Gurobi, CPLEX, Xpress and COPT pages describe the command-line solvers FlexTool runs, not the retired Python adapters, and give the native names the flags reach. Fix (docs): the 4.3.0 notes and the Gurobi page said --solver-time-limit (and --highs-threads) reached Gurobi; neither did before this release (--highs-threads still does not), and a misspelt Gurobi parameter does not raise an error (see above).

    Release 4.3.0 (11.11.2026) — AI assistant

    Behaviour changes — read before upgrading

    • Rolling solves of a unit, connection or storage node whose existing is a Map by period (without virtual_unitsize) now use the right unit size, so their rolling results can change; see "Solvers and rolling solves".
    • Gurobi now gets FlexTool's solver options (it solved at its own defaults before), so Gurobi results can change, for example a MIP stops at solver_mip_gap; see "Solvers and rolling solves".

    AI assistant

    • Fixed: a chat in the AI assistant window changed model after the settings changed. An open or reopened chat took the model saved later for new chats at its next question (and that model's level). A chat now keeps the service and model that answered in it, and its level unless you change the level of that same model; new chats use the saved settings. The top of the window shows the selected chat's service, model and level and where its data goes. The token line is now at the bottom of each chat, always in view, for the last question and the whole chat in thousands ("26k"); the terminal rounds its token line the same way. One empty row now separates "Png settings…" from "AI assistant…" in the side menu.
    • New: assistant levels, safety caps and the free plan. The AI assistant now places each model at a level (basic, standard or advanced) from its name and its context size; the window's settings and --tier offer only that level or a lower one, and say why (a model FlexTool does not recognise may go higher at your own risk). Each level has safety caps on the steps and tool calls for one question, and the context size sets how much a tool result may hold and how often earlier results are shortened; a request that would not fit the model's context is shortened first and never sent over it. The new free plan setting ("Free plan" in the settings, --free-tier on|off|auto in the terminal) makes each question use fewer steps, to stay within a free plan's request limits; it is on by itself only for OpenRouter models whose name ends :free, and the chat suggests it after a service first limits how fast questions can be sent. In the terminal, -q with a --tier above the model's levels exits with code 2 and sends nothing; the start of the chat shows the level and why, and /context the context size and the result size. get_entity and list_entities also return the entity's description. See "Assistant levels" in "The AI assistant".
    • New: the AI assistant in the FlexTool window. "AI assistant…" in the side menu opens a chat window for the open project; ⚙ beside it opens the assistant settings (service, address, key, model, level, privacy settings sent with each request, how many chats may wait for the service at once, how long conversations are kept). The first use explains how the assistant works; nothing is sent until you send a question, and before the first question goes to a service on the internet (or an Ollama cloud model) the data notice asks you to accept. The window says where the data goes. Each question also tells the assistant what is selected in the FlexTool window and the result viewer. Chats are numbered tabs: several can answer at once, they can be renamed, closed, reopened and deleted from the Chats menu, and the open ones come back when the window opens again. Closing FlexTool stops the answers and keeps what was received. Renaming a project moves its saved chats, deleting it deletes them. The window and the terminal chat share settings, keys, accepted notices and conversations. An agent connected with flextool-assistant --mcp sees the window's selections through get_gui_context while the project is open in the window. See "The AI assistant" in the documentation.
    • New: chat with an AI model about a FlexTool project in the terminal. flextool-assistant --project-path <project folder> asks your questions to the AI service you choose — OpenAI, Anthropic, Mistral, OpenRouter, Google Gemini, a model on this computer with Ollama or LM Studio, or any service with the OpenAI request format — and gives the model the same read-only tools as the agent connection. The first start explains how it works and sets up the service, key and model; keys come from the service's environment variable, the system keyring (--set-key) or, by choice, a key file. Before the first question goes to a service on the internet (or to an Ollama cloud model) the chat shows what is sent and that FlexTool cannot guarantee how it is used, and asks you to accept; the acceptance is asked again when the service, its address or the proxy changes. A model on this computer needs no acceptance. -q "question" answers one question for scripts (only the answer on stdout; --accept-data-notice accepts the notice for good). Conversations are kept per project for 30 days (--continue), the tokens used are counted as each service reports them (--usage), and Ctrl-C stops an answer at once. New requirements: anthropic, openai, keyring; run "Update FlexTool" (or pip install -e .). See "The AI assistant" in the documentation.
    • New: connect an AI agent to a FlexTool project. flextool-assistant --mcp --project-path <project folder> starts read-only tools that an AI agent such as Claude Code uses to answer questions about the project: input data by scenario, results (filtered, summed or averaged where that is meaningful, and compared between scenarios), run logs and slack, and the FlexTool documentation with the model's equations. For Claude Code: claude mcp add flextool -- flextool-assistant --mcp --project-path /path/to/project. The tools cannot change anything, read results only from a scenario whose latest run succeeded, and run on one thread by default (--threads N). Everything they return is sent to the AI service the agent uses; check its policies yourself. Three prompts set the assistant level (basic, standard, advanced); --tier basic offers only the basic tools. flextool-assistant --call TOOL '{...}' runs one tool in the terminal. See "The AI assistant" in the documentation. New requirements: mcp (2.3 or later 2.x), platformdirs, jsonschema; run "Update FlexTool" (or pip install -e .) to get them and the new command.
    • How the assistant answers. At every level the assistant gives the answer first. A question about one value gets the value, its unit and where it is stored, and nothing more. A why-question gets the cause in plain words, the few numbers that show it, the other causes it checked in one line, and what you could change, each option with its effect and drawback. It names entities, parameters, result tables and documents, never its tools, and adds a caveat only when it changes the conclusion.

    Parameter descriptions and result labels

    • Changed: every parameter description starts with its unit, in the default convention (MW, MWh, CUR, tCO2): [MW], [CUR/MWh], [hours]; [-] for a share or ratio, followed by an example ([-] e.g. 0.9 means 90%); words where the unit depends on the use ([unit of the constraint per MW]); [choice], [yes/no], [name], [text], [count] for the other parameters. Units that were wrong or incomplete are corrected: penalty_inertia is [CUR/MWs per hour] and penalty_reserve [CUR/MW per hour] (both are paid for each hour of the shortfall); fixed_cost and penalty_capacity_margin are per year ([CUR/kW/a], storage [CUR/kWh/a]) and annual_flow is [MWh/a]. One spelling per unit ([tCO2/MWh], [CUR/tCO2], [hours]). inflow, peak_inflow and energy_margin_adder say they are MWh per time step, when you must rescale them for another step length and when FlexTool does it; max_flow_for_unconstrained_variables no longer mentions commodity tiers (it does not bound them). Upgrading a database adds the units to its descriptions. Values and results are unchanged.
    • Fixed: result labels. node_inflow__dt, nodeGroup_flows_dt_g and the "Timestep inflow" of nodeGroup_gdt_p are MWh per time step (labelled MW before); entity_annuity_d_p is the discounted investment cost per MW (labelled as a yearly annuity before); the capacity factor tables unit_outputs_cf_d_ee, unit_inputs_cf_d_ee and connection_cf_d_e now carry their metadata (a ratio).

    Runs: records, logs and the GUI

    • New: every run records itself in its output folder. output_parquet/<run>/_run_status.json says whether the run is running, succeeded, failed, was interrupted, killed or crashed, with each solve's outcome (solver status, accepted / rejected and why, objective), the error of a failed run, the input database and scenario, the options and threads used, and whether the parquet files in the folder belong to this run or to an earlier one (a failed run keeps the earlier run's files). _run.log next to it holds the run's console output; a long log keeps its first 64 KiB and its last 4 MiB (FLEXTOOL_RUN_LOG_MAX_BYTES). Writing outputs again keeps both files. A run started from Spine Toolbox or with python -m flextool.cli.cmd_run_flextool records the Python console output; native solver lines and crash dumps are not in that log.
    • New flags --input-source-file PATH and --run-launcher NAME for run_flextool: the file the input database was made from, and who started the run, both recorded in _run_status.json.
    • Changed: flextool-run runs the engine as a child process and writes the run's _run.log, so the log also holds the solver's native output, a --save-memory solve's output and a crash dump. Arguments and exit code are the engine's, and Ctrl-C stops the run as before (a second Ctrl-C kills it). The process that stays for the whole run is small (about 20 MB, no polars). On a processor that needs the compatible polars build, flextool-run asks before it starts the engine. The GUI, the workflow runner and the calibrator (also the representative-period iteration) write the log the same way; a run that crashed or was killed is recorded as such. Output with characters outside the Windows console code page no longer crashes these runs. Update FlexTool (flextool-update, or pip install -e . in a source checkout) to get the new flextool-run; until then it runs the engine in the same process as before. The documentation and the command the GUI shows for a run use flextool-run.
    • Changed: the GUI lists failed and running runs with their state, and offers only results of a run that succeeded. A scenario whose latest run is running, failed, was interrupted, killed or crashed shows ✗ or … in place of its checkbox (– when the run wrote no parquet), says why on hover, and cannot be checked for plots or comparison, even when the folder still holds an earlier run's parquet; ▶ on it opens the run's log. The result viewer and the comparison leave such folders out. The list updates when a run starts. Results from before this version show as before.
    • Fixed: two GUI runs of the same scenario could run at once into the same output folder, the second deleting the first one's work folder. A run now waits until the earlier run of its folder has finished.
    • Fixed: stopping a GUI run left its --save-memory solver process running; it now stops the run's whole process tree.
    • Fixed: timings.csv and the output-info database ignored an output-location from the settings database while the outputs went there. All three now use the same location, as an absolute path.
    • Fixed: a run whose last check found a solve without an accepted solution exited 0 and wrote the output-info database (for example a Benders solve that found no feasible incumbent, or a run with no solve at all). It wrote no outputs, yet Spine Toolbox and the comparison steps read its finish_time as a finished run. Such a run now exits 1 and leaves the output-info database unchanged. A calibration no longer takes the outputs of an earlier run for those of a Benders run without an incumbent.

    Solvers and rolling solves

    • Rolling solves: unit size of a period-Map existing. A unit, connection or storage node without virtual_unitsize whose existing is a Map by period got a unit size of 1000 MW in the rolls of a rolling solve (single solves were right), so a 17 MW unit counted as 0.017 units. With startup_method = binary such a unit could never be online, and a min_instant_flow floor that needed it made roll 1 infeasible. The unit size is now the largest existing over the periods in every solve, as for a constant existing. Rolling results of such models can change.
    • Warning for a fractional unit count with binary start-ups. A unit or connection with integer online variables whose existing capacity is not a whole number of units in a period (for example existing dropping from 17 to 8.5 MW without virtual_unitsize: 0.5 units) now gets a warning naming it, the count and the unit size.
    • Gurobi options are applied. gurobi_cl takes parameters only as Name=value arguments; it ignored the ReadParams=<file> argument FlexTool passed ("Unknown parameter: 'ReadParams' (ignored)") and solved at its defaults. Each parameter of the merged options (solver_config/gurobi.opt, solver_mip_gap, --solver-time-limit, solver_arguments) is now passed as its own Name=value argument, so Gurobi results can change (for example a MIP now stops at the requested solver_mip_gap instead of Gurobi's 1e-4).
    • Licence message only for licence errors. A commercial solver that returned no solution was reported as a licensing problem whenever its output mentioned a licence, which every Gurobi run does (Using license file ...), so an infeasible model got a misleading message. The message now needs an actual licence failure in the output (no licence found, expired or not valid, failed to obtain a licence, size-limited licence, token server errors).

    Release 4.2.2 (8.11.2026) — time steps other than 1 h: new_stepduration and inflow scaling fixes

    These bugs affect models whose time steps are not 1 h, solves with new_stepduration, nested solves where a solve with new_stepduration runs before one without it, and scale_to_annual_and_peak_flow. Other models give the same results as in 4.2.1.

    • new_stepduration on time steps other than 1 h. A solve's new_stepduration now combines the right time steps for any step length (0.5 h, 2 h, mixed): inflow is summed, other time series are averaged (weighted by duration when the combined steps differ in length), and a constant inflow counts once per combined time step. Before, it read one row per hour of the new step, so on 2 h steps a constant inflow doubled and time series were misplaced, and on 0.5 h steps half of the energy was lost.
    • new_stepduration must fit the time steps. A new_stepduration that is not a whole multiple of the time steps (also 1.5 on 1 h steps, which silently made 2 h steps before), or a new step that would end inside a time step, now stops the run with a message naming the solve and the timeline. Only solves the model runs are checked.
    • scale_to_annual_and_peak_flow now meets annual_flow on time steps other than 1 h, with periods on different timesets and with representative periods of unequal length (it assumed 8760 steps a year; on 2 h steps the annual energy was 36 % short). Each period now reaches its own peak_inflow (the peak was taken over the whole timeline, so periods missed their peak). With new_stepduration, peak_inflow is matched per original time step. The input check's reach warning counts the time steps in a year.
    • Stochastic solves: scale_to_annual_flow and scale_to_annual_and_peak_flow work in branch periods (they stopped with an error).
    • scale_in_proportion with new_stepduration: a constant inflow is scaled as it is without new_stepduration (it came out about 2/3 of that on 2 h new steps).
    • Nested solves: a solve without new_stepduration that runs after one with it scales scale_to_annual_flow over its own time steps (it used the earlier solve's total). Results of such nested models can change.
    • max_flow_for_unconstrained_variables below 1,000,000 is now used as given (it was raised to 1,000,000).

    Release 4.2.1 (8.11.2026) — polars 2 supported again

    • polars 2 is supported again, with the memory of polars 1. polars 2.x reorders chains of joins by its own size estimate, and on the LP hand-off to HiGHS it picked an order that built a far larger intermediate table: peak memory about tripled on large models (the same LP), so 4.2.0 held polars below 2. polar-high 3.12.3 runs these joins in the order they are written, so FlexTool now requires polar-high 3.12.3 and accepts polars 1.40 or newer below 3.

    Release 4.2.0 (7.11.2026) — coarse time steps for units and connections, stochastic realization redesign, multi-solve cost & retirement fixes, run thread budget (schema v77)

    A large release: units and connections can decide on coarse time steps, stochastic solves are rebuilt around forecasts and a separate realization dispatch, and many result fixes change what existing models report. Highlights (the detailed entries follow):

    • Upgrade: opening a database migrates it to database version 77 automatically (value types match what FlexTool reads, group invest_method not_allowed becomes not_active, dropped parameters are listed). Runs on an upgraded database print a behaviour-change notice naming the affected entities; delete its metadata marker to silence it.
    • Coarse time steps for processes: units and connections whose nodes are all in new_stepduration groups decide once per coarse step: flows, online variables, reserves, exact connections and DC power flow. Coarse nodes now balance the sum of their flows (hourly-to-daily conversion no longer gets free energy).
    • Run thread budget: --threads N / FLEXTOOL_THREADS for FlexTool's processing, HiGHS and Benders; the GUI's "Cores per job" is now "Threads per run"; terminal and Toolbox runs process data on at most 8 threads.
    • Stochastics: a fan holds forecasts only; a realized: yes branch adds a realization dispatch; normal outputs hold realized results only (forecast rows in the output_horizon raw dump); per-path CO2, ladder and flow caps.
    • Construction lead time: invested is the capacity coming online; new in_construction column and constraint_in_construction_capacity_coeff.
    • Costs and retirement: a run of several solves reports costs from the start of the run, each once; annualized investment and fixed costs are yearly amounts; retirement counts until the end of life; new user-constraint constraint_capacity_coeff / constraint_retired_capacity_coeff.
    • Storage: default storage_start_end_method is fix_nothing; fix_end / fix_start_end pin the end; self-discharge is exact for any step length; bind_within_period and bind_intraperiod_blocks fixed.
    • Other important fixes: node and reserve prices in currency per MWh; reserve provision in MW; one N-1 constraint per failing unit; ramp limits scale with the step length; exact start-ups; unit commitment no longer links across gaps; CO2 limits in rolling and chained solves; inflow scaling stops with an error instead of dropping demand.
    • Removed / changed options: --precision-digits and --matrix-file-format (and their environment variables) removed; the GUI's MIP-gap setting removed and GUI solver settings now match terminal runs; new --benders-workers and --basis-cache-dir.
    • Dependencies: polar-high ≥ 3.12.2, highspy ≥ 1.15.1, polars held below 2 (2.x triples peak memory); Python 3.11+, CI on 3.13 and 3.14.

    • Platform: the Windows Toolbox bundle runs Python 3.14 with FlexTool installed, needs no Visual C++ runtime and is smaller; processors without AVX2 are supported through flextool[compat].

    • New: --threads N (FLEXTOOL_THREADS), one thread count per FlexTool run, for FlexTool's data processing and the solver. FlexTool's own processing (polars) uses at most N threads and at most 8 (POLARS_MAX_THREADS can set a lower limit), HiGHS gets threads=N (its parallel simplex stays off) and a Benders solve runs at most N region solves at once. Default: the available cores, with HiGHS and Benders as before. Give each run about cores / runs when several run at once (a script or an AI agent driving the terminal, Spine Toolbox running scenarios in parallel: add --threads N to the FlexTool Tool's arguments). The run header prints the threads in use. The workflow runner and the calibrator take --threads too and pass it on. Requires polar-high 3.12.2.

    • Changed: the GUI's "Cores per job" is now "Threads per run" and also sets FlexTool's own data processing. GUI runs used one thread for FlexTool's processing by accident, about 30-45 % slower before the solver; a GUI run now sends its "Threads per run" as --threads (default 1, as before). It no longer turns on HiGHS' parallel simplex when set above 1.

    • Changed: terminal and Spine Toolbox runs use at most 8 threads for FlexTool's data processing (before: every core). No measured slowdown, and less contention when several runs execute at once.

    • Changed: a solve HiGHS could not certify as optimal (status "Unknown") is judged against its own feasibility tolerance. It is accepted as near-optimal when its largest relative primal infeasibility is within max(1e-6, 10 × primal_feasibility_tolerance) (before: 1e-6 whatever the tolerance) and its primal-dual objective error is within the accepted gap. A solve run with a looser tolerance (e.g. 1e-3) that stopped the run before now writes results; with the HiGHS default 1e-7 nothing changes. A set FLEXTOOL_ACCEPT_PRIMAL_REL still replaces the limit. Upgraded databases print a behaviour-change notice for each solve accepted this way.

    • Fixed: a solve that HiGHS could not certify (status "Unknown") failed with "returned no model status" when autoscale rescaled the LP. Writing the unscaled solution back into HiGHS cleared its status and counters before the accept/reject check read them, so the near-optimal check never ran and the real status was not reported. The check now uses the status and counters of the solve itself.
    • Changed: polars is held below 2. polars 2.x roughly triples FlexTool's peak memory on large models (the same LP); installs keep polars 1.x until that is resolved.
    • Fixed: after "Update FlexTool" in the Windows Toolbox bundle, moving the bundle folder broke FlexTool in processes started outside its flextool folder (python -m ..., a Jupyter console): the reinstall wrote the folder's absolute path into the bundled Python. "Update FlexTool" now points the install to the clone by a path relative to the bundled Python again, so the bundle can be moved or unzipped anywhere.
    • Fixed: on Windows, a database could not be deleted or replaced after a run in the same Python session (Spine Toolbox console, a script calling FlexTool): the run kept the input database, the output-info database and results.sqlite open after it finished. Every database FlexTool opens is now closed when it is done.
    • Fixed: on Windows, creating or migrating a database wrote garbled parameter descriptions (â†’ for →, â€” for —). The schema and template JSON files were read with the Windows code page instead of UTF-8; they are now always read as UTF-8.
    • Fixed: FLEXTOOL_HIGHS_THREADS was ignored by run_flextool. The --highs-threads flag always sent 1, which replaced the environment variable, so HiGHS ran on one thread. An unset flag now lets the environment variable through; a given flag still wins. The flags of the other options with an environment variable twin work the same way (flag > environment variable > database > default).
    • Fixed: a solve with Benders decomposition ignored its scaling setting. Its region subproblems were scaled with full, or with the scaling of the solve that ran before it in the chain. Each solve now uses its own scaling; --scaling / FLEXTOOL_SCALING still override it.
    • Fixed: a second run in the same Python session (Spine Toolbox console, a script) took its solver option files (highs.opt, gurobi.opt, ...) from the previous run's work folder instead of from solver_config/ or FLEXTOOL_SOLVER_CONFIG_DIR. A run no longer sets FLEXTOOL_SOLVER_CONFIG_DIR, and run_flextool no longer writes its flags into environment variables.
    • Changed: an out-of-range numeric flag is ignored with a warning, like its environment variable. --solver-time-limit 0 (or a negative value) used to reach HiGHS as a zero time limit, which stops the solve at once, while FLEXTOOL_HIGHS_TIME_LIMIT=0 was ignored. Both forms now warn and fall through to the next setting (no limit by default). The same holds for a negative --solver-mip-gap and for --highs-threads 0.
    • Docs: --warm-start also works without --save-memory. It reuses the HiGHS basis in-process too; the help and the documentation said it worked only with --save-memory.
    • New flags --benders-workers N and --basis-cache-dir PATH for run_flextool, the command-line twins of FLEXTOOL_BENDERS_WORKERS and FLEXTOOL_BASIS_CACHE_DIR.
    • GUI runs now use the same solver settings as terminal and Spine Toolbox runs (behaviour change, notice). The GUI sends presolve and scaling only when you pick them in the Solver options dialog, whose new "Default" entries send nothing: presolve is then on (before: the GUI always sent choose), and scaling is each solve's scaling (choosing "Full" now forces full over the solve's setting). The GUI no longer has a MIP-gap setting: each solve uses its solver_mip_gap, else mip_rel_gap in solver_config/highs.opt (before: 0.001 for every solve). Results of MIP solves can change within the gap. A project saved by an earlier GUI is updated once when opened: a stored presolve choose and scaling full become "Default" (a stored presolve on / off is kept), and the GUI shows the change once. --solver-mip-gap and FLEXTOOL_HIGHS_MIP_GAP remain for terminal and Toolbox runs.
    • Removed --matrix-file-format and FLEXTOOL_MATRIX_FILE_FORMAT (and the GUI's "Matrix file format" setting). They had no effect: HiGHS is called in-process, or with an MPS file under --save-memory, and commercial solvers are always given an MPS file. A GUI project that stored the setting opens as before.
    • Removed --precision-digits, FLEXTOOL_PRECISION_DIGITS and FLEXTOOL_REPORT_NEAR_DUPS. They never affected a normal run (only --region passed the rounding on), so results do not change. FlexTool does not round input values; the developer documentation said it rounded them to 10 significant figures by default, which was wrong.
    • The run header lists the options set by a flag or an environment variable, with where each came from, and the number of polars threads.
    • New page "Environment variables" in the user guide: every variable users set, with its run_flextool flag twin and the order flag > environment variable > database > default. The developer guide keeps the diagnostic, profiling and experimental variables, now marked developer-only, and the help of every run_flextool flag names its environment variable.
    • Processors with AVX2 keep polars' fast build when the compatible build is installed too. polars loads its compatible build (polars[rtcompat]) first whenever it is installed, so a flextool[compat] install ran about 10-30% slower on every processor. import flextool now selects polars' default build on a processor that has its features (POLARS_PREFER_PKG; a value you set is kept). On Linux a processor feature now counts only when both /proc/cpuinfo and the processor itself (cpuid) report it, so emulators are read correctly.
    • Fixed: python -m flextool.env_check crashed on a processor without AVX2 when the compatible polars build was missing, before printing anything. It now prints the diagnostics and the pip command.
    • Windows Toolbox bundle: runs on processors without AVX2 and on Windows without the Visual C++ runtime. The bundle's Python now has both polars builds (FlexTool picks the right one at start). The libraries it loads carry their own C++ runtime, so no Visual C++ Redistributable is needed.
    • Windows Toolbox bundle is smaller: no debugger (debugpy), no test suites of FlexTool or of the bundled packages, no C headers or import libraries, and no unused pyarrow, pywin32 and Qt parts. The Jupyter Console still works, without debugging.

    • highspy 1.15.1 or newer is required (was >=1.14.0,<1.15; now >=1.15.1,<1.16). From 1.15 the Windows highspy carries its own C++ runtime; highspy 1.14.0 crashed on import on Windows 10 1809 / Server 2019. The automatic fix for a crashing highspy now installs this requirement instead of downgrading to 1.13.1. Two test scenarios with several equally cheap solutions now report another one of them (same total cost). If a library reports "DLL load failed" on Windows, python -m flextool.env_check points to the Visual C++ Redistributable.

    • Fixed: FlexTool on processors without AVX2 (for example Celeron/Pentium laptops up to about 2021, Intel Core from before 2013, AMD FX). polars' default build crashes on these. The automatic fix installed polars-lts-cpu, whose last release (1.33.1) is older than the polars FlexTool needs, so the fixed install was still broken. FlexTool now installs the compatible build of the same polars version (polars[rtcompat], about 10-30% slower), which polars loads in preference to the default build. It reads the processor's features when FlexTool is imported, before polars can crash. On such a processor without the compatible build, it prints why (the missing CPU features) and the exact pip command; in a terminal it asks "Run it now? [y/N]" and installs only on yes, and without a terminal (a Spine Toolbox tool, a GUI-started run, CI) it stops with that message instead of installing anything. The GUI (flextool, flextool-gui) asks the same before it loads polars, so the default build cannot crash first: in its terminal, or, started from a shortcut, in a small yes/no dialog with the reason and the command and a progress window while it installs; on no it closes with the message. "Update FlexTool" (flextool-update) installs it; an install that has polars-lts-cpu is moved to polars[rtcompat]. A processor without even SSE4.2/POPCNT (x86-64-v2, for example Core 2 or Phenom II), which no current build of FlexTool's libraries supports, now gets a message that says so. New install option pip install "flextool[compat]"; python -m flextool.env_check reports the processor's features and the installed polars builds; set FLEXTOOL_NO_ENV_CHECK=1 to skip the checks. The install pages now list the system requirements (64-bit Windows 10 or 11, Linux or macOS; an x86-64-v2 processor, AVX2 recommended).

    • Windows Toolbox bundle: Python 3.14, with FlexTool installed in the bundled Python. Before, the bundle's Python (3.12) had only pandas and spinedb-api, so FlexTool 4 tools could not run in it (polars, highspy and polar-high were installed into the build machine's Python instead). The bundle build now also runs an import check, a solve of the base example and a headless start of Spine Toolbox.
    • Python 3.14 is supported and tested (CI matrix now 3.13 and 3.14 on Linux, Windows and macOS; 3.11 is still checked by the nightly CI as the minimum version).
    • Fixed: results changed slightly from run to run with polars 2.x (row order of output files, last digits of discount factors, costs and duals). polars 2.x runs some queries on its streaming engine, which does not keep row order and adds partial sums in a varying order. FlexTool now selects polars' in-memory engine (the default of polars 1.x) when it is imported; a POLARS_ENGINE_AFFINITY set in the environment is respected.
    • Fixed: inflow scaling silently dropped demand or turned it into supply (models that now stop with an error ran with a wrong demand before). inflow_method scale_to_annual_flow, scale_in_proportion and scale_to_annual_and_peak_flow now stop with an error naming the node, method and period when the period has no annual_flow (or a negative one), when scale_to_annual_and_peak_flow has no (or a zero) peak_inflow, when the node has no inflow profile (or one that sums to 0) to scale, and when peak_inflow has the opposite sign of the inflow. Before, the node's inflow silently became 0 in such a period, and scale_to_annual_and_peak_flow without an inflow profile gave a constant supply of annual_flow / 8760 (for example +1027 MW for 9 TWh of demand). An explicit annual_flow of 0 still gives no inflow in that period. A warning (once per node) says when annual_flow is above |peak_inflow| x 8760, or when the peak scaling makes the inflow change direction at some time steps (annual_flow too small for the peak and the profile). The example database's ev and scale_to_peak_flow alternatives and the demand how-to's scale_to_proportion scenario lacked annual_flow: the EV battery now uses its inflow as given (use_original; before, its demand was 0), scale_to_peak_flow gets annual_flow 9 TWh (before, the demand of west was 0) and scale_to_proportion includes the scale_to_annual alternative.
    • Fixed: reserve provision results in MW (process__reserve__upDown__node__dt, ..._d_average, SpineDB reservation_t / reservation_average). The reserve a unit or connection holds is now reported in MW in each time step. Before, it was in units of the process's unit size (for example 0.02 instead of 20 MW for a 1000 MW unit) and, per time step, also multiplied by the step duration, so it was wrong for any unit size other than 1 MW and for steps other than one hour.
    • Fixed: the summary's total cost for realized periods (summary_solve.csv). The line "Total cost (calculated) realized periods" now sums only the periods a solve realizes (each once, discounted from the start of the run); before, it repeated the full-horizon total, which also holds the fixed cost of existing capacity in the look-ahead periods of the last solve.
    • Fixed: the per-period discounted costs were not written to CSV (output file names change). The per-period table of discounted costs is now costs_discounted__d.csv (Excel sheet costs_discounted__d); before, it and the horizon total shared the name costs_discounted, so the CSV writer kept only the horizon total and the Excel sheets were costs_discounted (per period) and costs_discounted_1 (total). costs_discounted.csv / the sheet costs_discounted is the horizon total by cost category, as before in CSV. The VRE share of a node group is group_node_VRE_share__d per period and group_node_VRE_share__dt per time step (before, the per-period table carried the __dt name and the per-step table none). The CSV writer now stops with an error naming both outputs when two outputs would be written to the same file (also when the names differ only by case).
    • Fixed: investment costs inside a stochastic fan (results change for stochastic solves that invest with a construction_lead_time or with stochastic_invest_method = recourse). The investment cost and the fixed cost of the new capacity that operates in a fan's branch periods are reported, weighted by each branch's probability weight, in the fan's period where the capacity starts to operate, also without a realization solve. A shared investment is reported at its full amount (its branch weights sum to 1); under recourse each forecast's own investment is reported times its branch weight, so the reported investment costs equal the investment part of the objective. Before, an investment whose capacity starts to operate in a later period of a fan without a realization solve (a construction lead time) was reported nowhere, and under recourse no investment cost was reported at all.
    • A warning when reported costs are rebuilt without the run-level cost data (a CSV-dump reload): such costs are discounted from the start of each solve, not from the start of the run, and the annualized investment, retirement and fixed costs of new and retired capacity are 0. The documentation now also says that the investment duals (invest_marginal) stay discounted from the start of each solve, like the objective.
    • Fixed: retiring existing capacity before its end of life (results change for models that retire capacity). Only capacity that existed before the model run (existing) can be retired, and a retirement now counts until that capacity's end of life: for reinvest_choice / no_investment the first year of the run plus lifetime, for reinvest_automatic the end of the model horizon. Before, a retirement counted in every later period, so past the end of life the retired capacity was subtracted a second time: the capacity went negative and a required retirement made the model infeasible (an optional one never happened); later solves of a chain subtracted earlier retirements from every period, and could be infeasible too. The capacity retired cannot exceed the existing capacity of any period it counts in. The retirement saves exactly the fixed cost the retired capacity would have paid in those periods (each period's fixed cost, discounted as the fixed cost of existing capacity; before, the retirement period's fixed cost with the investment discount, and over the lifetime from the retirement). Its salvage value is unchanged: an annuity over lifetime from the retirement period, counted inside the horizon as an investment's annuity. An entity with a retire option but no existing capacity has no retirement variable (a warning names it, once per run); a required retirement (retire_min_period, retire_min_total) that the existing capacity cannot supply is an error naming the entity and the period. An upgraded database prints a notice naming the affected entities.
    • Fixed: the end of life of existing capacity in a chain of solves and with stochastic branches in the first period (results change for reinvest_choice / no_investment existing capacity). It is the first year of the model run plus lifetime in every solve. Before, a later solve of a chain counted the lifetime from its own first period, so the existing capacity lived longer there and its reported fixed cost continued, and stochastic branches in the first period multiplied the lifetime by the number of branches (covered by the same notice).
    • Fixed: in a stochastic solve without recourse, capacity invested in a later period reaches the stochastic branches of that period (results change for stochastic solves whose stochastic_invest_method is not recourse and that invest or have a reinvest_choice / no_investment lifetime). The branch periods of the stochastic branches now count from the year of their period. Before, every branch period counted as the first year of the solve, so an investment decided in a later period, or one that starts to operate in a later period after a construction_lead_time, was not available in that period's branches (the model ran on penalties instead), and lifetimes ended late in the branches. An upgraded database prints a notice naming the entities.
    • Fixed: the annualized investment, retirement and fixed costs of new and retired capacity are yearly amounts in every period the capacity operates (cost_annualized of the model, units, connections and nodes; results change for models that invest or retire). The annualized investment & retirement, fixed cost invested and fixed cost reduction of divestments of a period are now the yearly annuity and fixed cost of every vintage operating in the period (and of every retirement that applies in it). Before, they were the discounted cost of the whole lifetime divided by the discount factor of the period where it was reported, so they appeared in that one period only and were many times too large there.
    • A run of several solves reports its costs from the start of the run, each once (results change for models with several solves in model.solves: chains of investment solves, investment and dispatch solves, stochastic and realization solves). The discounted costs of every solve now count their years from the first period of the run (before: from the first period of the solve, so a later solve's costs were too large by the inflation of the years before it). The investment and fixed cost of new capacity cover the years it operates in the whole run (before: only the deciding solve's periods) and are reported once, in the period the capacity starts to operate, by the solve that realizes that period; capacity carried from an earlier solve and retired capacity are no longer charged again as existing capacity. The fixed cost of existing capacity is also reported in the last solve's look-ahead periods, which the fixed cost of new capacity covers. A single solve reports as before, except that a lump of new capacity with a construction_lead_time is now in the period it starts operating and the "Investment discount factor" of the solve summary is now the investment factor (before: the operations factor). The printed objectives and the CO2 prices stay those of each solve. An upgraded database prints a notice listing the solves of such a run.
    • Fixed: the ramp of a unit with online variables at a stochastic branch point (results change for stochastic solves with mid-horizon branches and units with online variables and a downward ramp limit). Each branch's first step now reads the shut-down share of the trunk's last step once; before, it was multiplied by the number of branches squared (1.04 instead of 0.26 with two branches), which let units shut down at the branch point drop their flow faster than the ramp limit allows. An upgraded database prints a notice naming the units.
    • Units and connections between coarse nodes use the coarse step (results can change for models with a group new_stepduration; before, every flow, unit commitment and reserve stayed at the solve's step). A unit or connection whose balance nodes are all in coarse groups now decides once per coarse step, so the model gets smaller (the three-region hydrogen test model: 7 instead of 168 flow variables per liquefier and pipe arc in a week). Requires polar-high 3.12.1.
    • Flows: one flow per coarse step, a rate that stays the same over the step. Its capacity, availability, profile, efficiency and input-share limits apply to their average over the coarse step, and ramp limits apply between coarse steps. The result is the same as with flows at the solve's step when each flow has at most one limit that varies within the step and its cost, efficiency and prices do not vary within the step; otherwise the result can differ (hourly flows could use the cheapest or most efficient hours, and several varying limits are looser when averaged).
    • Units with online variables (startup_method linear or integer): units online, start-ups and shut-downs are decided once per coarse step (a unit starts or stops at the start of the step); minimum load and online capacity apply to the average over the step; a minimum up or down time counts whole coarse steps (a start-up counts while the steps since its start last less than min_uptime, so a 30-h minimum on daily steps holds the unit for two days, and one of at most a day adds nothing). With ramp limits, the start-up and shut-down capacity reduction uses the coarse step: a unit started at the start of a step is limited to its mean output over the whole step while ramping up from minimum load (at full load for the rest of the step once the ramp reaches it), likewise before a shut-down, and its units online ramp between coarse steps.
    • Reserves: nodes of a coarse group may now be in reserve groups (before, such a model stopped with "participates in reserves but is a member of resolution-group"). A provider holds one reserve per coarse step; the reserve requirement is still checked at every time step of the solve. Its reserve limits (the share of its capacity, a connection's net position, the floor of its flow, the storage energy for reserve_duration when the storage node and every provider are coarse) apply to the average over the step, with the average availability or profile (a wind unit offers reserve against its mean capacity factor over the step), and the room its ramp limit leaves for reserve is the smallest of the step's time steps (each with its own capacity factor).
    • Exact connections and DC power flow: an exact connection chooses its direction once per coarse step, its direction limits applying to the average over the step. A DC line has one flow per coarse step; the nodes of a DC network whose every line uses the coarse step (and whose nodes all have the same coarse step) have one voltage angle per coarse step. In a DC network with a line at the solve's step every angle stays at the solve's step, so that line keeps its per-step physics.
    • Rows that combine several units and connections: a capacity-margin, inertia or non-synchronous row of a group, a flow group's instant-flow limit, a user constraint with flow or storage terms, the DC power-flow row of a line and the stochastic storage-use tie of a node are one row per coarse step, the average of the row over the step, when every unit and connection in the row uses the coarse step; the inertia and non-synchronous shortfalls are then one value per coarse step. Such a row uses the step mean of the demand, inflow, profile, limit or constant in it: an averaged capacity margin covers the mean net demand of the step, not its peak hour. A row whose limit is given on only part of a coarse step stays at the time steps it covers. A row that mixes units or connections on the coarse step with others on the solve's step stays at every time step and sees the coarse flows constant through their step (a coarse DC line in a network with a line at the solve's step holds the angle difference across it constant through the step; the angles themselves stay per time step).
    • What keeps the solve's step: units and connections with a node outside the coarse groups, and those with a delay; flows that cross Benders regions or live in the Benders master; in a stochastic solve, a unit or connection at a stochastic node, with online variables or providing reserves, whose coarse step is tied between the forecasts over only part of it; in a realization solve, one whose committed units online or reserve change within a coarse step or are pinned on only part of it. An info line lists the units and connections that use a coarse step and those kept at the solve's step with the reason.
    • Outputs: flows, units online, reserves, voltage angles and the shortfalls of averaged rows are written at every time step of the solve, the same value through each coarse step; each start-up and shut-down is written whole at the first time step of its coarse step (0 at the others), so start-up totals and costs are the model's.
    • An upgraded database prints a notice naming each coarse unit or connection whose results can differ from flows at the solve's step, and why (a cost, price, efficiency or minimum load varying within the step, two or more varying limits, ramp limits, online variables, minimum times, the coarse start-up reduction, reserves and their step-mean headroom, a mixed row, or an averaged row whose demand, inflow, profile or limit varies within the step).
    • A Benders master that HiGHS left with an undetermined status after its cold retry is solved once more from its reloaded LP (polar-high 3.12.1), instead of stopping with "Benders master problem did not solve to optimality". This happened on badly scaled masters (an unscaled objective with cut slopes near 1e9).
    • The shipped how-to and example databases now carry the documented parameter defaults and value lists (for example lifetime_method, startup_method and storage_binding_method defaults, and the none members of the profile and ramp method lists), the same as a new database. Their data and results are unchanged.
    • Construction lead time: "invested" is the capacity that comes online (results change for models with a construction_lead_time). The capacity results (unit_capacity__d, connection_capacity__d, node_capacity__d) report as invested the capacity that comes online in the period, and a new column in_construction the capacity decided but not yet operating; the column is always written and is empty without a lead time, and capacity plots give it its own bar colour. Over combined solves each realized investment is counted once, in the period it comes online. The per-period investment limits (invest_max_period / invest_min_period, also of groups) and constraint_invested_capacity_coeff count capacity coming online in the period; the new constraint_in_construction_capacity_coeff puts the capacity in construction in a user constraint, and capacity in service = pre-built + invested − retired now holds with a lead time too. The annuity and fixed cost of new capacity are paid only while it operates, at the invest_cost (an overnight cost), lifetime and interest rate of the period it comes online; the lifetime starts then. The start of operation is rounded on the periods of the whole model, so a chain of solves and a single solve agree. Investments that would start to operate after the end of the solve or of the model horizon are not offered, with a warning naming the entity, the lead time and the solve; a chain whose solves are not longer than the lead time therefore does not invest in that technology (use a longer solve horizon or construction_lead_time_method immediate). A minimum per-period limit that nothing can meet (nothing can come online in the period) is an error naming the entity, the period and the lead time. An upgraded database with a lead time prints a notice naming the entities. Without a lead time, results are unchanged apart from the new empty column. A lead time in a solve whose invest_periods is a map (one investment sub-solve per period) stops with an error that explains why (capacity with a lead time could never come online inside a one-period sub-solve) and names the solve, the entities and their lead times; use one investment solve over several periods instead.
    • Dispatch and other time-series plots again leave a gap at timeline breaks: after a gap in the timeline, at the start of every representative period (also between two adjacent representative periods) and at the first step of a period that does not continue the previous one (also a later year that reuses the same timeset). Since version 4 no breaks were found, so every plot drew a straight line across them. Rolling windows that follow each other on the timeline stay one line. A break now applies to its own period only (before, a break time cut every period with a step of that name). The breaks are written to output_parquet/<scenario>/timeline_breaks.parquet (columns period, time); results saved with an earlier form of this file are drawn without gaps until the scenario is run again.
    • Output files come out in the same row and column order on every run. The rows of some set outputs (for example edd_invest, period_in_use and d_realize_invest) and the entity columns of some per-entity outputs could change order from one run to the next; a rerun of the same model now writes identical files. The values are unchanged, apart from the last digit of some sums whose terms were added in a varying order.
    • A map whose index depth or level names do not fit its parameter is now an error naming the entity, the parameter and what the parameter takes (for example a 2-level commodity.price, or a ladder tier level named period); before, such values were dropped without a message. Maps without index names at different depths within one parameter are read per depth: a one-level map keyed by time steps next to a period x time map is now read over time (before, its values were lost; results change, and an upgraded database prints a notice naming the entities). A one-line note says when a map's shape was read from its depth or keys; name the index levels to make it explicit.
    • Price-ladder tier labels are kept as written. Tiers such as 01 or a are now their own tiers. Before, a label was read as a whole number: tiers 01, 02, ... were bought at no cost, 1 and 01 were merged, and tiers like a were dropped (results change; an upgraded database whose ladder has such labels prints a notice naming the commodities). A tier with a quantity but no price is now an error naming the commodity, period and tier (before: price 0). A tier without a quantity stays unlimited.
    • A unit whose min_load map has no value above 0 in a solve's periods no longer stops the solve ("feature 'min_load' is active but ... p_min_load"), and it gets no minimum-load constraint rows in that solve. A reserve whose reservation adds up to 0 over a solve's time steps (for example a period map with values only for other periods) gets no reserve variables in that solve, as on the CSV path. Results of solves that ran before are unchanged.
    • A solve without period_timeset that uses the model's only timeset now gets the same periods everywhere.
