A price_ladder_annual given without periods now applies in every period of such a solve when the model has no periods_available either; before, it was skipped with a warning (results change; an upgraded database prints a notice naming the commodities). A multi-period stochastic solve without period_timeset now has the forecast-branch period names of its later periods too.
    • A model with no timeset now runs on the whole timeline instead of stopping with "More than one timeset available". With one timeline, FlexTool creates the timeset full_timeline covering it and uses it for every period of the solve, as documented. With several timelines and no timeset the error now says that no timeset is available.
    • --csv-dump shows period + time maps of node availability, penalty_up / penalty_down and self_discharge_loss. The dumped solve_data/pdtNode.csv listed the default value for such a map instead of the authored one. Only the debug dump was wrong: the model already used the authored values, so results are unchanged.
    • Lower peak memory in large in-process HiGHS solves. The LP's column and row names are no longer copied into HiGHS (a few GB on large models); they are passed only when a HiGHS option writes a file that carries them (write_solution_to_file, write_model_to_file, write_basis_file, …), so such files keep the real names. Autoscale's coefficient bucketing keeps its per-column tables as arrays. Results and outputs are unchanged. Requires polar-high 3.12.0.
    • The default storage_start_end_method is now fix_nothing. Storage nodes without their own method start free. Before, where some node had a storage_state_start, the old default fix_start fixed them at the start: at their own storage_state_start, or at 0 when only other nodes had one. The database upgrade writes fix_start on the nodes that have their own storage_state_start, so their results are unchanged (a notice names them). The other nodes now start free (results change; a notice names them). Where no node had a storage_state_start, the old default fixed nothing and results are unchanged.
    • fix_start / fix_end / fix_start_end without storage_state_start / storage_state_end is now an error naming the nodes (before: the start or end was silently not fixed, or fixed at 0). The database upgrade rewrites such nodes where it can tell what they did (fix_nothing, or a start / end value of 0) without changing results, and lists them; the error remains only where scenarios used the node differently.
    • Behaviour-change notices. When a release changes what an existing model does, the database upgrade marks the database (metadata flextool_behaviour_change:<change>, in the database editor's Metadata tab) and runs print a notice naming the version, its publication date, what changed and the affected entities, once per run. Deleting the marker stops the notice; new databases have none.

    • dispatch_realization_method = separate_dispatch without a realized: yes branch is now an error naming the solve (before: it was accepted although there was no branch to realize).

    • Database version 77: the value types of the database match what FlexTool reads. The investment and retirement limits per period of units, nodes, connections and groups, co2_max_period, startup_cost and the user-constraint constant can now be a constant or a period map; efficiency, min_load and efficiency_at_min_load of a unit, efficiency of a connection, profile, self_discharge_loss and energy_margin_adder also take period + time maps. Ten parameters that had no allowed value type (for example penalty_method, scaling, shared_operation_periods) now have one, so the database editor offers them. The upgrade only adds types; stored values are unchanged. Parameter descriptions were rewritten as short statements of what each parameter does.

    • A user-constraint constant can vary by period. A period map gives each period its own right-hand side; it must cover every period of the solve (a missing period is an error naming the constraint). Constraints with a constant value are unchanged.
    • Groups have their own invest_method values: not_allowed is now not_active (the default: the group sets no investment or retirement limits). The upgrade renames stored group values; a group value not_allowed in a database that was not upgraded stops the run with an error. Unit, node and connection values are unchanged. Results unchanged.
    • Group retirement limits in the database. retire_max_period, retire_min_period, retire_max_total and retire_min_total of a group (with a retire option in its invest_method) were read by FlexTool but missing from the database; they are now there.
    • New user-constraint parameters constraint_capacity_coeff and constraint_retired_capacity_coeff on units, nodes and connections. constraint_capacity_coeff puts the capacity in service in each period (existing plus investments still in service minus retirements, the capacity that limits the flows) on the left side of a user constraint; constraint_retired_capacity_coeff puts the capacity retired in each period there. Shares of the fleet, ratios between technologies and replacement rules can now account for retirements.
    • The pre-built capacity term of user constraints subtracts earlier retirements (results change). constraint_cumulative_pre_built_capacity_coeff is now the capacity in service at the start of each period: existing capacity plus earlier investments still in service minus the retirements of earlier periods. Before, retirements decided earlier in the same solve were ignored while those of earlier solves of a chain were subtracted, so one solve and a chain of solves of the same problem gave different results. Results change only for models that retire capacity of an entity with this coefficient; upgraded databases print a notice naming the constraints. Capacity in service = pre-built + invested − retired, also with a construction lead time (see the construction lead time entry above).
    • User constraints with only capacity terms are written once per period instead of once per time step (the rows were identical). In the output_horizon raw dump these rows are process_constraint_{equal,less_than,greater_than}_period with dimensions (constraint, period).
    • A user constraint with no term left in a solve gets no row. This happens when its coefficients only refer to flows, storage states, investments or retirements that the solve does not have. When the remaining row cannot hold (for example 0 >= 5, or one period of a period-map constant), the run stops with an error naming the constraint, the solve and the period. Before, the solve was infeasible without naming the constraint. When the row always holds (for example 0 >= 0), a warning names the constraint once per run.
    • Fix: a user-constraint pre-built coefficient now gives a zero-cost entity its investment variable (results change only for such entities). An entity without investment cost that a user constraint linked only through constraint_cumulative_pre_built_capacity_coeff got no investment variable, because the link was looked up under a wrong parameter name. The new capacity and retired-capacity coefficients link the investment and retirement variables the same way.
    • The upgrade drops parameters FlexTool no longer has (discount_years, max_relative_capacity, storage_include_solve_fix_method, unit input capacity_max_coeff / capacity_min_coeff) and lists any values they held; an input capacity_max_coeff value moves to input_share_max (its name since database version 70) where that input has none of its own. solve_mode single_shot becomes single_solve. Results unchanged.
    • Fix: DC power-flow lines are always the connections inside dc_power_flow_with_angles groups (results change for DC power-flow models that also have an is_DC connection; a notice names the groups). Such a model used its is_DC connections as the DC power-flow lines: the group's lines got no angle constraints and the reference node could change. An is_DC connection inside such a group is not a line; it keeps its own transfer_method. A startup_method on an is_DC connection no longer raises the DC power-flow error, and the "DC power flow per connection" output of a model without DC power flow is now empty (it listed the is_DC connections; solved values unchanged).
    • Fix: a node retirement or an investment without its period map stops with an error naming the missing data (before: the retirement or investment was left out of the capacity without a word). Results unchanged.
    • Warning when existing is not a whole number of units for a unit or connection with integer online variables (startup_method = binary) and virtual_unitsize. With virtual_unitsize, existing is the number of units; integer online variables commit whole units, so a count such as 2.5 cannot all be online. The warning names the entity, the count and the unit size. Results unchanged.
    • Docs: with virtual_unitsize, existing is the number of units (as in FlexTool 3 and every 4.x release); the 4.x reference example said MW. Results unchanged.
    • Fixed: net-load representative-period clustering reads existing as the number of units with virtual_unitsize (results change: the selected representative periods can differ for a VRE unit with virtual_unitsize other than 1 and a non-zero existing, when the representative periods are selected again). The existing VRE capacity is now existing × virtual_unitsize, as in the model; before, the clustering used existing as MW. The tool prints a note naming such units. Stored selections are not changed.
    • Fixed: the --region export gave a cross-region connection with virtual_unitsize only existing MW. The virtual half-flow connection of the region's input now carries existing × virtual_unitsize. Solve results (including the Benders decomposition) are unchanged.
    • Fixed: the automatic DC power-flow reference node reads a node's existing as the number of units with virtual_unitsize. The reference is the node with the largest existing capacity (existing × virtual_unitsize); before, existing was compared as is, so a node with virtual_unitsize could lose to a smaller one. Results change only where that picks another reference node.
    • Docs: a lossy connection's capacity limits the flow it delivers. The how-to example on the regular transfer method said both nodes can send 500 but receive 400 (capacity 500, efficiency 0.8); as the model has always done, they receive 500 and send 625. Results unchanged.
    • Docs: constraint_invested_capacity_coeff is a coefficient per MW (MWh for storage). Its description said "Not multiplied by unitsize"; FlexTool has always applied it to the invested capacity in MW (MWh).
    • Docs: increase_reserve_ratio applies to upward and downward reserve alike. The ratio stands for the variability of a flow, which can go either way, so each participating flow raises both requirements (unlike large_failure_ratio, which is directional). The reference and the parameter descriptions now say so; the model is unchanged. The reference also lists the database version 77 parameter shapes, the group retirement limits and the group invest_method value not_active.

    • Fix: Excel export keeps period + time maps of parameters without stochastic values. Such a value (for example a period + time energy_margin_adder) was dropped when a model was exported to xlsx; it is now written on its own multi-index sheet and read back on import. Behaviour-change markers of a database (its metadata) are now also written to the workbook and restored on import.

    • Fix: memory is returned to the system when polars was imported before flextool. FlexTool sets the memory allocator of polars to give freed memory back to the operating system about a second after it goes idle, but the setting only took effect when flextool was imported first. In a Spine Toolbox console where an earlier tool had imported polars, in a notebook or in a script that imported polars first, freed memory stayed with the process and peak memory on a large model could be about twice as high. The setting is now also applied when polars is already loaded, and memory freed before flextool was imported is returned as well. Where it cannot be applied (for example on macOS), one warning names the remedy: import flextool before polars, or start Python with _RJEM_MALLOC_CONF=dirty_decay_ms:1000,muzzy_decay_ms:0.

    • Lower peak memory when building the non-synchronous limit of a group. The constraint no longer combines the unit size of every process with every time step before picking the processes that feed the group; on a large model this removes several GB from the peak of the scaling step. The model is unchanged.

    • Fix: the online commitment no longer links across a gap in the timeline. The units online at the first step after a gap were compared with the units online at the step before the gap, so a start-up (or a free shut-down) was counted across the gap. The first step of a solve or of a period that does not continue the previous one wrapped to the period's last step when that step name existed in the solve, and otherwise started from zero units online (a start-up charged at the first step of later periods). Now every step without an adjacent predecessor — the first step of a solve or such a period, the step after a gap, the start of a representative period — takes the units online at the last step of its own stretch of the period (the steps that follow each other up to the next gap), as a solve with one block always did. The commitment thus cycles within each block and never reaches across a gap, a representative-period start or into another period, the same as ramp limits and the minimum up / down time look-back. In a stochastic solve that branches mid-horizon, the steps before the reveal cycle among themselves.

    • Fix: the units online carried into the next roll stop at a gap, a new year and a representative-period start. A rolling or nested solve took over the units online realized at the end of the previous roll at its first step whatever came between them: across a gap in the timeline, into a new year that reuses the same timeset, at a representative-period start, and, when no step matched, on the first step of the solve anyway. The commitment is now carried only to the steps from which ramp limits and the minimum up / down time continue (the steps that continue the previous roll's last realized step on the timeline, including a continuing period, every forecast branch starting there and a solve with longer time steps). Elsewhere the first step cycles within its own stretch of the period, as within a solve. A carried commitment without the step it was realized at now stops with an error instead of being placed on the first step.
    • Fix: a solve with longer time steps continues the finer dispatch realized before it. In nested and rolling solves, the ramp limit and the minimum up / down time carry of a solve's first step were taken over from the previous solve only when the step before it on its own timeline was the very step realized last. A solve with longer steps than the dispatch realized before it (for example a 6-hour storage roll after an hourly dispatch) never matched, so its first step had no ramp limit from the realized flow, the start-ups realized just before it did not count towards min_uptime / min_downtime, and a warning said the look-back was cut short. The two steps are now matched by where they end in time on the underlying timeline: when the step before the first step ends exactly where the realized step ends, the solve continues it (the ramp uses the realized step's own duration). A hole or an overlap between the two still gives no carry and the warning.
    • Fix: the minimum up / down time obligation carried into the next roll follows the timeline. The start-ups and shut-downs realized near the end of a roll were carried into the next roll only within a period of the same name, matched by sorting step names. The forecast branches of a stochastic roll therefore never took the obligation (the history had the period's name, not the branch's), and neither did a period that continues the previous one on the same timeline. The carry now starts at the first steps that continue the previous roll's last realized step on the timeline — the same steps from which ramp limits continue — and counts the hours from each realized start-up or shut-down to them, so every forecast branch and a continuing period take it; a period that does not continue the previous one, a step after a gap and a representative-period start still take none.

    • Fix: minimum up and down times only look back along the timeline. The look-back of min_uptime / min_downtime walked the time steps in the order of their names, so it reached from a forecast branch into the last hours of a sibling branch, from the first hours of an investment year into the end of an earlier year that reuses the same timeset, and across gaps in the timeline and the starts of representative periods. It now follows the same adjacent-time links as ramp limits: it continues into a period whose time steps continue the previous period's on the same timeline and into the steps of a forecast branch before the reveal, and stops at a gap, at the start of a representative period, at a period that does not continue the previous one and at the first step of a solve (no cyclic wrap). A rolling or nested solve whose first step continues the timeline of an earlier solve, but not the last step realized before it (so no realized on-line history reaches it), now prints a warning naming the process.

    • Fix: co2_max_total in rolling solves and chains of solves no longer allows more than the limit. Each roll or solve subtracted the emissions earlier rolls and solves had already realized as plain tonnes, while the limit counts emissions annualized and times years_represented. The history was therefore under-counted by years_represented / period share (52 times for a one-week period that represents a year), and a rolling or chained run could emit several times its limit. The realized emissions are now counted the same way as in the limit, so the realized emissions of the whole run stay within it. The per-forecast-path co2_max_total constraints of rolling stochastic solves (below) use the same corrected budget.

    • A recourse stochastic fan nested inside another solve now stops with an error. A solve with stochastic_invest_method = recourse that is nested (contains_solves) in a solve sharing exactly one of its periods ran without the ties that keep its forecast investments non-anticipative. It now stops with a configuration error naming the sub-solve; make it a top-level solve.

    • A committed window in a forecasts-only fan no longer stops a run over a cap on another period. The check that stops a solve committing steps inside a fan nobody realizes named the annual commodity-ladder tiers and co2_max_period caps of every period; it now only counts caps on the fan's own period.

    • Fix: in a rolling stochastic solve each forecast keeps its own cumulative limits. The rolls of a rolling solve were built without their scenario paths, so the CO2 total cap (co2_max_total), the cumulative commodity-ladder tier cap and the flow-group cumulative flow limits (max_cumulative_flow / min_cumulative_flow) pooled all forecasts of a roll into one constraint: the forecasts shared one budget (the ladder tier allowed the forecasts' hours added together, the CO2 cap counted every forecast's emissions against one budget). Each forecast now gets its own constraint, as in a single stochastic solve. The cumulative tier cap of a scenario path also counts the periods that earlier solves or rolls already finished, so a fan in a later period no longer gets a too-tight (or negative) budget. Deterministic rolls and realization rolls are unchanged.

    • Fix: in a stochastic fan the capacity-margin penalty is weighted by the forecast's probability. Each forecast branch period of an investment period keeps its own capacity-margin shortfall, but its penalty was counted in full, so a fan of forecasts paid the shortfall as many times as it had branches regardless of their weights. Each forecast's penalty is now multiplied by its branch weight, as the operational costs already were. Deterministic solves are unchanged.

    • Fix: in a rolling stochastic solve, a forecast's period budgets count the hours already realized. The annual commodity-ladder tiers and co2_max_period allot each roll the share of the period reached by the end of its horizon. A forecast branch counted only its own window, not the hours the earlier realization rolls had already realized, so its tier budget shrank as the week advanced. After the realized purchases were subtracted it could turn negative and make the roll infeasible. Each forecast now counts its period's realized hours plus its own window. The co2_max_total share of a fan counts those hours once, and so does the fallback weight of the cumulative ladder. A forecast's co2_max_period cap also subtracts the emissions already realized in its period; before, every forecast could emit them again.

    • Normal outputs hold realized results only, with or without output_horizon. output_horizon = yes no longer changes the parquet / CSV / plot / summary outputs: forecast-branch rows, rolling look-ahead rows and per-forecast (recourse) investment rows are gone from them, and the "realized periods" / "full horizon" totals no longer include forecast-branch costs. Those rows are only in the raw dump under horizon/. A stochastic fan with no realization solve (forecasts only) reports its investment decisions only: without the option it also used to report the forecast branches' costs, prices and capacities. CO2 in the summary is one total for the realized periods.

    • Fix: node and reserve prices of a solve that realizes no time step are no longer reported. In a nested run, the investment and storage solves above the dispatch solve (and a forecasts-only stochastic fan) realize no dispatch step, yet their node prices reached node_prices_dt_e next to the dispatch prices, and the per-step price plots could show the parent's price for a step. Prices now come only from the solves that realize the step.

    • Fix: output_horizon = yes no longer changes the next solve. In a solve followed by another solve (rolling, realization dispatch, multi-solve), the CO2 emitted in unrealized steps, and the unrealized hours and commodity use, were carried into the next solve's CO2 limits (co2_max_period, co2_max_total), cumulative-hour totals and commodity ladders. Only realized steps are carried now, as without the option.

    • New: raw LP dump with output_horizon = yes. The debug option now also writes output_parquet/<scenario>/horizon/: one raw parquet per LP variable and constraint holding all rows of every solve (forecast branches and rolling look-ahead steps included), appended over the solves and rolls, with solve, roll and realized columns, raw primal values, reduced costs and duals. Not available for solves decomposed with Benders.

    • timeset_weights now reach the forecast branches of a stochastic solve, and a map indexed by time is no longer ignored. The forecast branch copies of a period (single, recourse and rolling stochastic solves) used weight 1 on every time step instead of the weights of their period's timeset; they now take that timeset's weights, so the objective, the energy and cost outputs and the prices weigh the branch steps like the deterministic period. A timeset_weights map whose index_name is time (or t) was read as no weights at all in a single solve; it now gives the same weights as a map without an index name. A timeset_weights value that is not a timestep map (a scalar, or a map indexed by period) is now a configuration error instead of being ignored. Objectives of affected solves change.

    • Units online are no longer tied across the forecast branches when no step is committed. With shared_operation_periods = [], or in a rolling solve whose fan lies only in the look-ahead, the units online were still held equal in every forecast over every time step of the fan, although the other operations were free. They are now tied only over the committed (tied) steps, like storage use and reserves, so each forecast can commit units on its own there. Objectives of such solves can be lower.

    • Under stochastic_invest_method = none, investment cost in a stochastic fan is no longer counted several times. The investment annuity, the salvage value of retirements and the lifetime fixed cost of capacity built before or at the reveal were summed over the period's original name and again over each forecast branch copy of the same calendar years, so with two forecasts the fan periods were charged three times. Each calendar period the capacity serves is now counted once, as under recourse. Objectives and reported investment costs of such solves are lower; the chosen investment can change (it was biased low).

    • A realized: yes branch in a stochastic solve's stochastic_branches now makes FlexTool run a realization dispatch right after the stochastic solve, without setting dispatch_realization_method = separate_dispatch. It applies when the designated branch opens a fan that reaches the periods the solve realizes (a single or rolling solve with stochastic_invest_method = none). The realization dispatch is deterministic, fixes the stochastic solve's investments, reads the designated branch's data, and inherits the units online and the reserves the stochastic solve committed before the forecast is known (the value of the first forecast branch in sorted order over the tied steps; reserves were not inherited before). The stochastic solve keeps those ties over the window it hands to the realization dispatch. A deterministic solve placed right after the stochastic solve in model.solves, with the same solve mode and rolling settings and realizing the same fan periods, is used as the realization dispatch instead. A designated solve that contains other solves or is nested in another solve is a configuration error; under stochastic_invest_method = recourse a warning says that no realization dispatch is added. When the inherited units online cannot cover the realized net load of a node with penalty_method = off, the run stops before that solve with an error naming the node and the time step.

    • Under stochastic_invest_method = recourse, cumulative_max_capacity / cumulative_min_capacity now apply to every forecast branch. The cumulative-capacity limit of a cumulative_limits entity sat on the period's original name, which has no investment under recourse: a cumulative_min_capacity made the solve infeasible, and neither limit constrained the forecast branches' investment.

    • A stochastic fan now has no dispatch of its own under the period's original name. The period that opens a fan (and every later period of it) keeps its name only for period-level bookkeeping: its investment (under stochastic_invest_method = none, seen by every forecast), years and discounting. Its operational results (flows, storage, units online, reserves, prices, costs per time step) are empty, not 0: those decisions exist only per forecast branch, and the realized operation comes from a realization dispatch (see the realized: yes entry above). Before, the original period name carried a weight-0 copy of the dispatch: an arbitrary feasible point that was reported, carried to the next roll and, without a designated branch, filled with a guessed scenario. Forecast branches stay visible through model.output_horizon. Under stochastic_invest_method = recourse the original period name has no investment either: every forecast branch invests on its own, and the shared-investment ties (shared_invest_periods) now join the forecasts to each other. A forecast branch's retirement now reduces that branch's capacity (before, a branch retirement was paid but changed no capacity), and every forecast of a fan in a solve's first period starts from the solve's start state (or the previous roll's carry), not from its own last step. Per-path caps and outputs (investment and cumulative-flow totals, co2_max_total, the cumulative price ladder) have no __anchor path any more: each forecast path holds the shared periods before the reveal and the fan's here-and-now investment.

    • A committed window that reaches into a forecasts-only fan now stops the run before the LP when a later solve continues from it and no solve realizes it: the error names the solve and the families decided per forecast there (storage state, ramp-limited flows, the cumulative commodity ladder, the CO2 total cap, finite annual commodity-ladder tiers, the CO2 period cap, recourse investment, or units online / reserves outside the tied steps) and suggests designating a realized: yes branch, configuring a realization solve, or ending the committed window before the reveal. Units online and reserves on the tied steps are the same in every forecast and need no realization: the next solve (e.g. the next roll) continues the units online, and the start-up / shut-down history of minimum up and down times, from them (before, it continued from nothing at all, as if no unit had been online).
    • A forecasts-only fan (no realized: yes branch) that authors a stochastic-time value (availability, efficiency, min_load, operational cost, reservation, penalty, profile) is valid again: every forecast branch period takes its own branch's value. A designated realized: yes branch's value feeds the realization dispatch. An entity in no include_stochastics group reads the realized branch's value on every branch period, so in a forecasts-only fan such a value is a configuration error naming the entity and the parameter (it would otherwise silently become the class default); add the entity to the group, designate a realized branch, or give a non-stochastic value. The same holds for the reservation of a reserve group without include_stochastics (before, it silently became 0). A stochastic-time profile is read per profile: when any of its users is in an include_stochastics group, all its users read the forecast branches' own series. The how-to stochastics example 2_day_stochastic_dispatch is a forecasts-only fan of four forecasts again, so it reports no realized dispatch (the forecast branches are visible through model.output_horizon); 1_week_rolling_wind keeps its realized branch.

    • A stochastic-time value that gives only some of its forecast branches a number, leaving others out, is now a configuration error naming the entity, the parameter and the missing branches, instead of silently treating the branches left out as the class default. A deterministic solve that authors a stochastic-time value takes the realized: yes branch's value and raises a configuration error when no branch is designated, instead of silently using the class default (a realized branch projected from elsewhere — e.g. for a rolling solve's realization dispatch — is used, not an error).

    • A rolling stochastic solve's commodity price-ladder tier cap now gives a forecast branch period the same already-bought carry-over as its anchor, instead of re-opening the tier's budget in full for every branch. The running "already bought this tier" total carried from roll to roll is only ever recorded under the anchor's real period name; a branch period of the same calendar year had no row of its own there, so its remaining budget silently fell back to the un-reduced full-tier amount — too loose — while the anchor's own row correctly subtracted what earlier rolls had bought. Branch periods now read their anchor's carried-over amount the same way they already read its tier price and quantity. A deterministic solve, a single (non-rolling) stochastic solve, or a rolling solve without a price ladder is unaffected.

    • A rolling solve that auto-spawns a separate realized-dispatch solve now carries the commodity price-ladder's rolling "already bought" total forward from that dispatch solve alone, instead of also adding the same hours' purchases from the solve that spawned it. The spawning solve's own dispatch for those hours is a placeholder with no weight in its objective (the dispatch solve re-solves the same hours for real right afterwards), so its own purchase split across tiers was arbitrary; folding it into the running total on top of the dispatch solve's own, correct contribution double-counted those hours and could leave an arbitrary residual in the carried total besides. The spawning solve now carries the running total forward unchanged, leaving the addition to the dispatch solve that actually realizes the hours. A rolling solve that does not auto-spawn a separate dispatch solve, or that has no price ladder, is unaffected.

    • The same auto-spawned realized-dispatch solve fix applies to the rolling CO2 cap's "already emitted" total, for the same reason: the spawning solve's own emissions for the hours its dispatch solve re-solves were a placeholder, and adding them to the running total on top of the dispatch solve's own, correct contribution double-counted those hours. A rolling solve that does not auto-spawn a separate dispatch solve, or that has no CO2 cap, is unaffected.

    • A stochastic branch period now inherits its anchor's existing capacity and fixed cost, instead of only the anchor seeing them. A period map keyed by the anchor period (e.g. existing or fixed_cost set for just that one period, the normal way to author a value that applies to the whole forecast fan) used to reach only the anchor: every branch period fell back to zero existing capacity, and the fixed cost of existing capacity — when that optional objective term is turned on — silently dropped out of the objective because the only period left to weight was the anchor's own zero-probability placeholder. Branch periods now read their anchor's value the same way every other period-keyed input already does. A deterministic solve, or a value authored as a plain constant, is unaffected.

    • A group's per-period investment or divestment cap now applies to a stochastic branch period the same way the entity's own cap already did. Under the recourse investment feature, a branch period is itself an investment period, and the entity-level cap already applied there — but the group-level cap's row was missing for branch periods, so a group member could invest or divest past the group's limit in every branch, constrained only by its own (looser) individual cap. A deterministic solve, or a solve without the recourse feature, is unaffected.

    • Fixed a follow-on bug from the group per-period cap fix above: a branch period where no group member actually invests or divests (for example because its own authored value of zero wins over the anchor's) no longer makes the solve infeasible. The cap's row now only applies where a group member actually has the investment or divestment there; previously the row still carried the group's minimum-per-period floor at a branch with nothing to satisfy it, forcing an infeasible zero-against-floor comparison instead of leaving the branch unconstrained. A deterministic solve, a solve without the recourse feature, or a group cap without a minimum is unaffected.

    • A non-recourse stochastic solve's operating costs now get the same years_represented weight as the investment side, instead of being weighted one year regardless of how many years the period represents. Every forecast branch period (the ones carrying the solve's probability weight) used to fall back to one year per period for p_inflation_op even when its anchor period represented several years, while the investment side was still annualised over the full span — under-weighting every operating cost relative to investment by the same factor. Branch periods now read their anchor's years the same way the investment annuity side already does. A deterministic solve, a rolling solve, or a stochastic solve with years_represented = 1 (every stochastic fixture in the regression suite) is unaffected.

    • A flow into a CO2-priced or CO2-capped node now nets against the group's emissions the way the solve does, instead of only when the same process both emits and removes at the same node. The removal credit's CO2 cost and emission outputs used to require the crediting flow to belong to the very process (and node) that also emits, so a removal process (e.g. direct air capture) distinct from whatever emits in the group went uncredited in the reports, even though the solve itself already gave it the credit. The credit is now attributed to the process that delivers it, and nets against the group's total the same way the LP objective and caps do. Models with no removal flow, or where removal and emission happen to be the same process and node, are unaffected.

    • The per-period CO2 price for a rolling solve now averages the rolls that realize a period, weighted by the hours each roll keeps of it, instead of only reporting the last roll's price. When a period's hours are split across more than one rolling window, each window's own shadow price covered only its own slice of the period; reporting just the last window's price silently dropped every earlier window's realized hours. A period realized by a single roll is unaffected.

    • The per-period CO2 price in a stochastic solve no longer comes out multiplied by the branch's probability. The per-period CO2 cap isn't weighted by the branch probability the way the objective's CO2 price term is, so the raw shadow price was the path's true price times its own branch weight — each path now reports its own price, as node prices now do (see "Prices are in currency per MWh" below). A deterministic solve is unaffected.

    • The model-wide and per-group CO2 totals in a stochastic solve now report each scenario path separately, instead of adding every forecast branch's emissions into one pooled number. The anchor's shared history and every branch's own periods used to be summed together as if they were one timeline, counting several alternative futures' emissions against a single total. A deterministic solve, or a stochastic solve with only one scenario path, is unaffected.

    • A group's whole-horizon CO2 cap and a commodity price ladder's cumulative tier cap now apply separately to each path of a stochastic solve, instead of being shared across every forecast branch. Both caps used to sum every forecast branch's emissions or purchases into a single row, so a solve with several alternative futures let them collectively spend several times the intended budget between them (one shared row instead of one per branch). Each cap now gets one row per scenario path: the whole-horizon CO2 cap keeps the same remaining budget on each path (the forecast branches are alternative futures, not consecutive years to divide the budget across); the commodity ladder's cumulative cap gives each path its own share of the budget, weighted by the periods that path actually covers. A deterministic solve, or a stochastic solve with no CO2 total cap or cumulative ladder tier, is unaffected.

    • A group's per-period and whole-horizon CO2 caps now carry a remaining budget across the rolls of a rolling solve and across the solves of a chain, instead of re-granting the full cap every time. Both caps used to recompute the allowed emissions from the fixed input cap on every roll / solve, with no memory of what had already happened, so a period touched by several rolling windows could be allowed its full per-window share on each of them — over-allowing roughly in proportion to the number of rolls or chained solves touching it. Each roll's / solve's cap is now "the budget allocated up to the end of this roll's horizon, minus what earlier rolls or solves already emitted" (the same mechanism the commodity price ladder's rolling budget already used), so the remaining room only ever shrinks by what was actually realized. A single, non-rolling, non-chained solve is unaffected. The internal CO2 accumulator that tracks realized emissions across rolls also now counts a min-load-efficiency unit's part-load fuel, which it previously missed.

    • A flow into a CO2-priced or CO2-capped node now earns a removal credit, instead of being ignored by the solve while the results already reported it as a credit. Modelling CO2 removal (e.g. direct air capture or bio-energy with carbon capture) as a flow into a CO2-content commodity's node, where that node belongs to a group with an active CO2 price or cap, used to cost and count nothing in the solve itself — even though the emission reports already netted the removal off the group's total, so the reported numbers and the solve's incentives disagreed. The flow now earns co2_content × co2_price as a negative cost (reducing the objective) and reduces the same group's emission caps, matching the emission reports and the documented "CO2 price is added to the price of the commodity" behaviour. Models that never route a flow into a CO2-priced or capped node are unaffected.

    • CO2 emission outputs now report physical emissions even when no group prices or caps CO2. The model-wide and per-unit/connection CO2 outputs used to come from the same flow set the LP builds for a priced or capped CO2 group — so a commodity with a co2_content row, but no group with an active co2_price or co2_max_period/co2_max_total on its node, reported zero emissions even while units were burning that fuel. Emission totals are now derived from the commodity's own flows whenever it has a nonzero co2_content, independent of whether a group happens to price or cap it; the per-group breakdown is unchanged and still requires a group that actually tracks CO2.

    • A commodity price ladder in a rolling solve no longer crashes the solve, and its annual tier quantity is now scaled to how much of the year the solve actually covers. The annual tier quantity is a per-year amount, but a short or rolling solve used to compare it against the un-annualised MWh bought in that solve, so a solve covering only part of a year could spend a whole year's cheap-tier allowance in that short window. The per-period cap is now scaled by the solve's own share of the modelled year and of the roll's progress through it, minus what earlier rolls already bought against that same allowance — so each roll's budget is exactly what the model has allocated so far, never more, never less. The cumulative price ladder's rolling carry used to be able to drive a later roll's remaining budget negative and crash the solve once a chained run moved from one modelled period into the next; the real cause was that the per-roll progress measure feeding the cap's running total silently dropped a period's contribution the moment the chain finished it, instead of keeping it. Both ladders' running totals now account for every period the chain has ever simulated, so the remaining budget can never go negative.

    • The cumulative price ladder's running budget now accounts for how much of a modelled year each period's own dispatch covers, and how many real years that period stands for. Like the annual tier's quantity, the cumulative tier's quantity is a per-modelled-year amount, but its running budget used to credit a period's full allowance for every period the chain touched, regardless of how much of a year that period's own simulated hours cover or how many real calendar years the period represents — so a multi-year or representative-sample chain could let the cumulative cap run far more permissive than intended, never actually binding. The budget is now scaled the same way as the annual tier's (by the period's own share of a modelled year) and further by the number of real years that period represents, so the two ladders' running budgets are on a comparable footing.

    • A commodity bought on a price ladder by a unit with a minimum-load efficiency curve now counts the extra fuel that unit burns at part load. The ladder's purchase balance used to count only the full-load fuel use, so such a unit's actual fuel draw could exceed what the ladder tiers had accounted for.

    • The cost outputs now show what a commodity price ladder actually cost. A commodity priced on a ladder used to be excluded entirely from the reported commodity cost (or, if it also had a flat price authored, priced at that flat price instead of what the ladder tiers actually cost) — so the reported operating cost could disagree with the solved objective. The commodity cost output is now priced from the ladder purchase at the tier price actually paid.

    • A multi-flow unit with availability below 1 can now reach its derated full output. The fuel (input) arc of such a unit used to cap its flow at availability times the full-load fuel need, which is tighter than the fuel the unit actually draws at derated full output whenever it has a minimum-load fuel section or outputs with different per-output coefficients — so the unit's output could fall short of availability times capacity even though nothing else limited it. The fuel arc's limit now matches the fuel the unit actually needs at that output level, so a unit with availability below 1 reaches its full derated output.

    • Period x time maps of unit/connection efficiency, min_load, efficiency_at_min_load and availability are now honoured in rolling, nested and stochastic-rolling solves, not only a single solve. A genuine two-dimensional map (period, then time within each period) of one of these parameters used to be read correctly only by a solve with no rolls or nested sub-solves; every roll or nested sub-solve fell back to the parameter's constant / fold default instead, changing the unit's or connection's efficiency, minimum load and availability (and so its fuel use, cost and feasibility) for the rest of the model run. A period x time map now gives the same result in every roll and sub-period as the equivalent single solve.

    • Fixed a related case where a period sharing a name with one of the model's own timesteps could lose an explicitly period-labelled map value on rolling / nested sub-solves.

    • A unit with a single input and a single output now honours its per-arc conversion_flow_coeff in its fuel use, cost and CO2, not only a multi-flow unit. The input/output coefficient on unit--inputNode / unit--outputNode used to be applied only on a unit with several input or output flows; on the common one-input-one-output unit it was silently ignored (the model solved to a byte-identical LP whatever the coefficient was set to). The coefficient is now folded into the unit's fuel-use curve, the same way a multi-flow unit already applies it, so a non-default coefficient now changes the reported input flow, fuel cost and CO2 of such a unit. A unit with a minimum-load efficiency curve and a non-default output coefficient should note that its authored efficiency / efficiency_at_min_load are then no longer exactly its energy efficiency at full or minimum load (see the updated conversion_flow_coeff documentation). A coefficient of 0 keeps its existing "uncapped edge" meaning rather than being folded in, now with a warning naming the affected unit.

    • Storage capacity retired in a period now stays retired in later periods. The storage state limits, the start/end and cyclic start bindings, the reserve headroom and the cumulative capacity limits now subtract every earlier retirement of a storage node, and the storage capacity outputs show it. Before, a storage node's retirement earned its salvage value without ever reducing the storage capacity bound, so the node could still be dispatched and cycled as if nothing had been retired, and in some configurations could be "retired" again and again for repeated salvage.

    • A flow group's net flow now counts the fuel a min-load-efficient unit draws while online, not only the fuel its output flow implies. flowGroup-based flow limits (cumulative-over- the-solve, cumulative-per-period and instantaneous) used to measure a flow group's net flow as output flow times slope only; a unit with a minimum-load efficiency curve also draws a fixed "section" fuel proportional to the capacity online, which the node balance, the CO2 price and the CO2 caps already count but the flow-group limits did not. The limits now use the same fuel measure throughout.

    • Flow-group cumulative flow limits now weight each time step the same way every operational cost does, instead of by its duration alone. max_cumulative_flow / min_cumulative_flow (per period and over the whole solve) used to multiply a step's net flow by its duration only; a representative-period step's own weight (and, for the whole-solve limit, how many years a period represents) used to drop out, so an under- or over-represented step counted as a full, unweighted hour. Under stochastic recourse, the whole-solve limit now applies separately to each scenario path instead of pooling every branch's flow into one row, so one branch's surplus can no longer cover another branch's deficit; this matches how every other per-scenario limit in the model already works.

    • An explicit invest_max_period / retire_max_period / invest_max_total / retire_max_total of 0 on a group now binds, instead of being misread as "no limit" once another group sets the same parameter. This was the same "an authored zero is a given value" gap already fixed for the entity-level versions, carried over to the group readers.

    • A cumulative_limits entity (unit, connection or storage node) with only a cumulative_min_capacity floor and no cumulative_max_capacity ceiling can invest again. Such an entity used to be pinned at zero investable capacity — forbidding the very investment needed to reach its own floor — because the internal ceiling used to default to 0 when nobody authored a maximum. It is now unconstrained above (the actual limit, if any, is still enforced by the cumulative-capacity constraint itself); an entity that also authors a cumulative_max_capacity is unaffected. The same gap also existed on the second (later) solve of a continuation/rolling chain and is fixed there too.

    • A group with a cumulative_limits investment method now gets the same "give your own maximum once a sibling sets one" check as an entity does. Once one cumulative_limits group sets cumulative_max_capacity for a period, another such group that leaves that period out of its own map used to be silently read as "no limit" there instead of being asked for an explicit value (or an explicit 0 to forbid investment); it now raises naming the group that needs a value, matching the entity-level behaviour.

    • Group cumulative capacity limits now subtract a group's process retirements, same as the entity-level limit (#29). A group of units/connections with a cumulative capacity limit (invest_method = cumulative_limits) ignored its own retirements entirely; the group's capacity in service only ever grew. Storage-node members are unaffected (the engine has no retirement history to draw on for them, same pre-existing gap as the entity-level limit).

    • Group cumulative capacity limits now read the documented cumulative_max_capacity / cumulative_min_capacity parameters. Groups previously needed undocumented invest_max_cumulative / invest_min_cumulative values (not part of the schema) to get a cumulative-capacity constraint; the documented, schema parameters were silently ignored. Also, which groups get a total/cumulative investment or retirement limit is now determined by each group's invest_method, matching the entity-level behaviour, instead of by "any group with a positive value of the matching limit parameter": a group with a limit value but a different (or no) method no longer gets rows it never asked for, and a group whose method does call for a total limit no longer loses its own row just because another group happens to set the same parameter.

    • Cumulative capacity limits now subtract every earlier retirement, not just a retirement of the row's own period. cumulative_max_capacity / cumulative_min_capacity on a unit, connection or storage node compare existing plus invested plus retired capacity against a limit for every period; a retirement from an earlier period used to drop out of that comparison again at later periods (processes/units only — storage nodes already counted every retirement before this fix and are unchanged). An explicit cumulative_max_capacity = 0 (a phase-out year) now binds as given, instead of being read as "no limit"; a period an entity's own map leaves out, once that entity authors the parameter at all, is now a configuration error instead of silently capping investment at zero there.

    • Investment and retirement totals over a chain of solves now count every earlier solve, not just the capacity still alive. invest_max_total / retire_max_total (and the group-level analogues) used to ignore everything a previous solve in the same chain had already built or retired, so a chain of solves could invest past its own cap. invest_min_total / retire_min_total used to re-impose the full minimum in every solve instead of crediting what earlier solves had already met, and a minimum on a storage node could be silently skipped whenever the matching per-process check had nothing to check first. Both now count the capacity the whole chain has built or retired so far and only require what is left; the minimum is now one requirement per entity (or group) over the whole chain — per scenario branch under stochastic recourse — instead of a repeated per-period check, so it can no longer be satisfied by pooling alternative branches together. An entity or group whose investment/retirement method still needs a per-period or total limit, but is never given one explicitly, now raises a clear configuration error instead of silently capping it at zero; a limit nobody sets at all is unaffected and stays unlimited, as before. The investment/retirement total's shadow-price output is now a single value per entity/group for the whole chain instead of one per period; a group's shadow price under stochastic recourse is no longer available in this output (it never was for the matching per-entity value either).

    • Startups and shutdowns are exact, and the ramp limit of a unit with online variables applies to its online capacity. Startups and shutdowns are now exactly the change in units online: declaring a startup or a shutdown that does not match a real change in committed units is no longer possible, and declaring one no longer buys ramp room or reserve room by itself. A unit with online variables now ramps on the units online in both steps, reaching at most its startup output in the step it starts and dropping from at least its minimum load; a start/stop pair with online unchanged can still gain a little room on an input or on one output of a multi-output unit, which its startup cost pays for. Ramp-limited unit-commitment models can cost more than before (the free ramp room is gone); objectives of unit-commitment models without ramp limits are unchanged.

    • Online units continue across consecutive periods of one timeline and across rolling windows from the right step. A unit with online variables used to be charged a spurious startup where a period's first step continued the previous period on the same timeline (two periods on consecutive parts of one timeline, or a forecast branch continuing from the step before its fan), and a continuation roll's carried commitment used to land on the timeline's alphabetically first period instead of the roll's real first step. Both are fixed; a unit that stays committed across either boundary is no longer re-charged a startup there.

    • A period or time Map with an unset/default Map index name (index_name) no longer drops other entities' values of the same parameter. When one entity's Map had no index name set (Spine stores this as x) and its keys could not be matched to either a period or a timestep of the active solve, FlexTool used to drop the whole parameter for every entity of that class, including other entities whose own values were set normally. Now only the one entity with no matching key is affected (it gets the parameter's usual default, exactly as if it had authored nothing); every other entity's value is unaffected. The most visible case was a storage's self_discharge_loss: one node's unrelated, unresolvable Map could silently zero out every other node's self-discharge loss. Setting the Map's index_name explicitly to period or time (see "How to define the temporal properties of the model" in the user docs) avoids the ambiguity entirely and is still recommended.

    • A commodity's price ladder (price_ladder_annual, price_ladder_cumulative) is read correctly regardless of how its Map levels are indexed. A depth-2 annual ladder (the same tier prices and quantities applied in every model period) was silently ignored, as was any ladder whose Map levels were left with Toolbox's default index name instead of being named tier / period explicitly. Two commodities authoring the ladder at different depths (one with a fixed annual price, one varying by period) could also have their tier numbers and period names mixed up with each other. Every one of these forms is now read correctly; naming a ladder's Map levels explicitly is no longer necessary.

    • A model with a single timeline and no explicit timeset_duration now gets its usual "the timeset covers the whole timeline" default reliably. FlexTool has always defaulted a timeset with no authored duration to the whole (sole) timeline, and a timeset with no authored timeline to that sole timeline, when the model otherwise leaves this unspecified. A model built without these defaults in mind (for example model_builder.build_model's generated models, which never write timeset_duration at all) could end up with several of its per-period weighting Params (period-share-of-year, inflation, timestep weighting, branch weighting, profile values) silently missing. Both defaults now apply consistently wherever the model's time structure is read, not only on the legacy CSV-based path.

    • A reused work folder no longer mixes old outputs in. Running FlexTool twice into the same work folder (for example, re-running in the same directory after a failed run or a --csv-dump run) could mix an earlier run's per-roll output files into the new run's results, producing wrong values or, in some cases, a crash. Every run now starts by clearing its own previous output stash in the work folder (the raw per-roll files and the --csv-dump debug mirrors); nothing else there is touched. The GUI and Spine Toolbox already gave each run its own folder, so this only changes behaviour for a command-line run reused in the same directory.

    • invest_marginal now shows the dual of an unbuilt entity, and group investment caps/floors reach their members. For a unit, connection or node entity that is eligible to invest but not built, invest_marginal (and the unit_invest_marginal__d / connection_invest_marginal__d / node_invest_marginal__d outputs) always showed 0 (or a near-zero -0.0), because the output writer looked up the entity's class (unit/connection/node) from a file a run never writes. That value is now read from the run's own data, so an unbuilt entity shows its true (negative) marginal value — negative there means it would be unprofitable to build, which is expected, not a numerical artefact (see the updated description in docs/results.md). A binding investment cap or floor on a group of entities is also now correctly spread to its member entities' invest_marginal, instead of only ever applying to the group itself.

    • Five debug-only solve_data/ files no longer leak into a normal run's work folder. p_entity_divested.csv, fix_storage_quantity.csv, fix_storage_usage.csv, p_roll_continue_state.csv and p_entity_period_existing_capacity.csv are solve-to-solve handoff mirrors that nothing in FlexTool reads back; they are debug output for --csv-dump only and are no longer written on an ordinary run. --csv-dump still writes all five, unchanged.

    • --csv-dump writes the per-solve input dump again. With --csv-dump (the GUI's "Full" debug level) each sub-solve's inputs were supposed to be dumped to input/ and solve_data/ in the work folder, but the setting never reached the solves, so only the end-of-run snapshot was written. Each sub-solve now dumps into the work folder (a later sub-solve overwrites the files of an earlier one); a decomposition=benders solve dumps its whole, undecomposed input. The dump also keeps the solve's objective scale in solve_data/scale_the_objective.csv, which the end-of-run snapshot used to replace with an empty file, so the offline output writer (python -m flextool.engine._csv_reload.offline_outputs) no longer needs --scale. A dump that fails is reported as an error with the solve's name; the run continues.

    • A database file left in the work folder no longer changes or crashes the run. When the work folder held a copy of a database named tests.sqlite (for example the run's own input database), the run read its entity names and switched the model's internal data types. The solve then built the same model in a different order, or stopped with a SchemaError (seen with rolling separate dispatch and nested fix_quantity runs). The run now takes everything from its input database and ignores such files. load_flextool now needs the run's data provider and database reader; loading a model from a CSV dump is done only by the unsupported flextool.engine._csv_reload package.

    • Leftover CSV files in a reused work folder no longer change the model. When a run found no value for an input in memory, it read a CSV file of that name from the work folder instead, if one was there (for example from an earlier --csv-dump of another model). A stale dump of a model with an exact connection made a regular connection of the next model exact as well. The model is now built only from the database of the run; CSV files in the work folder are ignored.

    • A run that cannot read its database stops with an error. When the input database could not be opened for the scenario (for example locked by another program or not migrated to the current version), the run went on without the database's settings and finished normally with a wrong model. It now stops with an error that names the database and the scenario. A run without a scenario also stops: the command line asks for --scenario-name (or, in Spine Toolbox, a scenario filter on the tool) instead of failing later with an unrelated message.

    • A nested fix_usage caps windows with no storage use. With storage_nested_fix_method fix_usage, a dispatch period whose matching storage-solve window had zero storage usage got no usage cap at all (and, with use_reference_value, the reference end value instead). It is now capped at zero usage like any other window.

    • Only the last period of a solve ends its horizon. In a solve with several periods that share a timeset, the end of every period was treated as the end of the solve: storage_solve_horizon_method use_reference_value pinned and use_reference_price valued the storage at the end of each period, and fix_end / fix_start_end pinned each period end of the last solve. These now apply only at the end of the solve's last period (and of each stochastic branch of it), as documented. In a rolling solve with bind_intraperiod_blocks, the end of a period that a roll holds only in part, followed by a period the roll holds whole, also gets the node's storage_solve_horizon_method, since nothing else continues from it.

    • The csv dump shows what each solve was built from. With csv_dump, handoff/*.csv now holds the carriers the last solve consumed (its starting storage state, running totals, …), so the dump and a model rebuilt from the run match the solved model; the solve's own end state and final totals are in produced_handoff/*.csv.

    • fix_start_end ignores the storage binding method. As documented, a storage node with storage_start_end_method fix_start_end now chains forward like bind_forward_only whatever its storage_binding_method. Before, a cyclic method (bind_within_timeblock, bind_within_period, bind_within_solve and their _blended_weights forms) kept cycling, so the start and the end were tied together, and with different storage_state_start and storage_state_end the solve needed slack or ended away from storage_state_end. A bind_intraperiod_blocks node keeps its blocks, which now chain forward from period to period instead of looping within each period.

    • fix_end / fix_start_end fix the end of the storage. The storage_state_end of a fix_end or fix_start_end storage was never applied: the storage ended wherever the model chose. The end state is now pinned to storage_state_end (a share of the storage capacity, invested storage included) at the last timestep of the entire model, in every solve whose horizon contains it: e.g. both an investment solve and the last dispatch roll when both reach the end of the model, each nested level's solve that does, each terminal branch of a stochastic solve. A solve or roll that ends earlier is not pinned. In a nested lower level, an upper-level fix_quantity or fix_usage of the same step takes precedence. storage_start_end_method is meant for simple models; storage_state_reference_value with use_reference_value is usually the better choice.

    • The horizon-end reference value yields to a nested storage fix. A nested dispatch solve with storage_solve_horizon_method use_reference_value and storage_nested_fix_method fix_quantity (the recommended nested setup) pinned the end of each roll both to the reference value and to the storage solve's state, and the solve was infeasible. Where the upper-level solve fixes, caps or prices the storage at the step, its value now overrides the reference value, as documented.

    • Nested dispatch rolls keep their unit commitment, ramps and running totals. In a nested solve every dispatch roll after the first took the roll-forward carriers from the storage or investment solve above it, which realises no dispatch: each roll restarted its unit commitment from its own cyclic wrap (charging startups again), ramped from nothing, forgot the min up / down time history and, with a cumulative price ladder, the tier energy already bought. They now continue from the dispatch realised before them, also across a later roll of the storage solve; a storage roll continues the commitment of the dispatch solved below it.

    • Later solves continue from the realised storage state. In a solve sequence (e.g. one solve per investment period) every solve was pinned again to storage_state_start, discarding the state the previous solve realised, and the first dispatch roll under a later roll of a rolling storage solve started with a free storage level (free energy). Now storage_start_end_method fix_start applies to the first step of the model and to the first appearance of each nested level; every later solve or roll starts from the latest storage state realised before it at its level or below (a storage or investment solve from the dispatch below it). Cyclic binding methods keep cycling within each later solve without the start value, and representative-period (_blended_weights) nodes start each block free.

    • fix_start of a bind_within_timeblock storage. The start state was pinned on the state at the end of the solve's first step, after that step's flows, so the timeblock's cycle could start at any level and spend the difference in the first step. It is now pinned at the step the first step starts from, the last step of the first timeblock (in a rolling solve, of the timeblock's part in the first roll), as for the other cyclic methods. Where an upper-level nested solve fixes the storage state at that step (a nested roll whose horizon is one timeblock), the fixed value wins.

    • Non-synchronous limit: internal flows, DC connections and reverse flows. The non-synchronous limit of a group counted the flows of a connection between two nodes of the group (both directions, as supply) in single solves, though not in rolling solves; the optimiser could cycle energy through such a connection, or through a storage connection inside the group, to loosen the limit. Those flows are now excluded on every solve path, as documented. An is_DC connection's flow into the group is counted as non-synchronous also when a unit is flagged is_non_synchronous (it was dropped then), and the reverse flow of a no_losses_no_variable_cost or DC connection is counted (infeed into the group, supply out of it). Models with a non-synchronous limit change (the non_sync_and_curtailment how-to: higher cost, the battery no longer cycles). A warning names a no_losses_no_variable_cost connection that crosses the boundary of a group with a non-synchronous limit (flowing both ways at once is free and loosens the limit; use exact).

    • The energy margin no longer enters the non-synchronous limit and the capacity margin. An energy_margin_multiplier or energy_margin_adder added its demand to the group rows of the investment solve too: the non-synchronous limit allowed more non-synchronous infeed (its supply side grew with the added demand) and the capacity margin asked for capacity above the margined peak. Both now use the real demand, as the reserve requirement already did; the margin enters only the node balance (set capacity_margin for a capacity surplus). Models with both an energy margin and one of these group limits change (in the test model the tighter non-synchronous limit moves generation from wind to coal). A --csv-dump of a solve with an energy margin now also writes pdtNodeInflow_unmargined.csv, so a reload keeps the real demand.

    • Reserve price of groups with several requirements. reserve_price_t is now the sum of the duals of all of a group's requirement constraints at the step (timeseries, dynamic, and the large-failure constraint of every failing unit or connection): the cost of one more MW of every requirement of the group at once. Groups with a large-failure requirement were not reported at all (large_failure_only, dynamic_and_large_failure) or reported only the timeseries dual (timeseries_and_large_failure, all), and timeseries_and_dynamic groups reported the timeseries dual alone, which is 0 when the dynamic requirement is the binding one. When a requirement is short the price is now penalty_reserve.

    • Time-varying self_discharge_loss. A self_discharge_loss given as a time (or period) map was read as one value per map entry and every coefficient of the storage's balance was summed over the entries (about 72× for a 72-step map): the storage behaved as if it were many times larger, with no error. The map is now read per period and time step: each step decays at its own rate, and a coarse (new_stepduration) step or a bind_intraperiod_blocks block decays by the sum of its hours' losses.

    • use_reference_value counts invested storage. The end state of a storage with storage_solve_horizon_method use_reference_value was pinned to storage_state_reference_value × the existing capacity only, so newly built storage had to end (nearly) empty. It is now that share of the built capacity (existing + invested − divested), as for the start state and the storage capacity limit.

    • fix_start_end fixes the start state. A storage node with storage_start_end_method fix_start_end got no start condition at all (it could start with free energy); its start is now fixed like fix_start. Its end is fixed too (see "fix_end / fix_start_end fix the end of the storage" above).

    • fix_start of a bind_within_solve storage in a solve with several periods. The start state was pinned at the last step of the first period, i.e. in the middle of the solve. It is now pinned at the step the solve's first step starts from: the last step of the solve (in a stochastic solve, the end of each forecast branch the first step wraps to).

    • Storage self-discharge is exact for any time-step length (for a loss that is constant within each step). The storage content now decays continuously at ln(1 + self_discharge_loss) per hour, whatever the step length, with the energy charged or discharged within a step spread evenly over the step. Before, a step lost self_discharge_loss once whatever its length (a 24 h step lost one hour's worth, so storages with self-discharge on steps longer than one hour kept far too much energy), coarse (new_stepduration) groups and bind_intraperiod_blocks blocks used a linear loss × hours that under-decays stored energy, and energy charged within a step was decayed for the whole step. Results with self-discharge change, slightly also at 1 h steps (the test battery's states by up to 0.0025 MWh). The reported self-discharge (balance_t Self discharge) now equals the loss the optimisation used; before it was state × loss per hour, which differed for steps other than 1 h, coarse nodes and blocks.

    • bind_intraperiod_blocks storage balance. The balance of such a node over each timeset block is now exactly its per-step energy balance summed over the block. Before, it left out the reverse flow of two-way connections that use one flow variable (no_losses_no_variable_cost) and of DC power-flow lines, and the part-load (minimum load) input of min_load_efficiency units drawing from the node. The reported storage state of a block is now the state at the end of the block (before: at its start), like the state of every other storage node, and with fix_start the start state is pinned at the end of the first period (the state the first block starts from). The node's price keeps its sign.

    • Cyclic storage in rolling solves. In the second and later rolls of a rolling solve, the first step of a bind_within_timeblock, bind_within_period or bind_within_solve storage node mixed the cycle with the storage level carried over from the previous roll (the start state was forced halfway between the two). Each roll now cycles on its own, as documented, and the carried level is not used for these methods. Results of rolling solves with these methods change (the fullYear_roll test model: total cost −1.1 %).

    • Blended-weights storage in solves without representative periods. A solve without representative periods treats a *_blended_weights node as the plain method (e.g. bind_within_solve_blended_weights as bind_within_solve). When another node used the plain method in the database, the converted nodes lost their storage chain (their energy could appear or vanish freely). They now keep it.

    • bind_within_period storage works. A storage node with storage_binding_method bind_within_period had no state-change term at all (its energy could appear or vanish freely) and no error was raised; the same happened to bind_within_period_blended_weights nodes in solves without representative periods (e.g. a chronological dispatch after a representative-period investment solve). The state now cycles within each period: blocks chain inside the period and the period's first step continues from its own last step, also in rolling, stochastic and coarse (new_stepduration) solves. With fix_start the start state is pinned at the last step of the first period (the step the first period wraps to). use_reference_value does not pin the end state of a bind_within_period node (nor of a blended-weights node a solve without representative periods turns into bind_within_period / bind_within_solve), as already for bind_within_solve / bind_within_timeblock: their end is tied to their start.

    • Representative periods in solves with several periods. Every period of a solve whose timeset has representative_period_weights now gets its own weights, representative blocks and base-period storage chain. Before, only the first such period did (unless a node used bind_within_period_blended_weights): the other periods' representative days were weighted 1.0 instead of their weights, so their operating costs were under- or over-counted, and the bind_within_solve_blended_weights / bind_forward_only_blended_weights storage of later periods lost its base-period chain. Periods sharing a timeset, or base-period names on one timeline, shared one chain and weight set. Objectives of multi-period representative-period models change (the test models by −45 % to +74 %; compared with the hourly model of the same days the error drops from 3–100 % to under 0.3 %). The across-solve variants now chain the periods in solve order; different blended-weights methods may now share a solve. A solve that mixes representative and other periods with a blended-weights node, a stochastic solve on a representative-period timeset, and a rolling solve with blended-weights nodes now stop with an error (their representative data was not defined).

    • Benders with in-out stabilisation reports a valid upper bound. With benders_in_out_weight > 0 the decomposition could stop on an upper bound below the true optimum (by up to 3e-4 on the three-region hydrogen test model): the two regions at the ends of a pipe could be solved with different flows on it. Both ends now always get the same flow, and the upper bound is the cost of that one point, and the investments handed to a following dispatch solve are the plan that bound priced. This costs one extra master solve per iteration (iteration counts are unchanged on the test models).

    • Solver options reach every solve. solver_mip_gap was ignored on the default (warm) HiGHS path; it is now honoured. A solve with solver_arguments or solver_mip_gap that runs cold (warm=False, --save-memory) no longer loses solver_config/highs.opt, the CLI flags (--highs-threads, --solver-time-limit, …), the determinism defaults and the automatic scaling options; every HiGHS solve now gets the same layered set. Benders decomposition now uses these options too (master and region subproblems; see the solver docs for the details), including a thread count (--highs-threads, or threads in highs.opt) with parallel regions.

    • Stochastic storage tie counts every flow into the node. The tie that keeps the net flow into a stochastic storage node equal in all forecasts before the reveal now also counts the reverse flow of two-way connections that use one flow variable (no_losses_no_variable_cost) and of DC power-flow lines, and the part-load (minimum load) input of min_load_efficiency units. Before, forecasts could move energy in or out of the node through those flows differently, i.e. use knowledge of the future.

    • Prices are in currency per MWh. price_t is now the cost of one more MWh of demand at the node and step (positive when more demand costs more). Before, node prices were negated and multiplied by the period's share of the year, by the timestep weight and, in stochastic solves, by the branch probability (e.g. −164 250 instead of 900 for an unserved-energy step). Reserve prices (reserve_price_t) were a million times too small and are now per MW of reserve held for one hour, also with the step length and weights removed. In a stochastic solve each forecast branch now reports its own price, and a step the objective does not weigh (e.g. a step that timeset_weights leaves out) has no price. The nested fix_price storage handoff passed the dispatch solve a price a million times too small (and weighted); it now passes the storage solve's price_t to the dispatch solve, also to the rolls of a rolling dispatch solve (before, a rolling dispatch solve always received 0) and also for bind_intraperiod_blocks storage nodes.
